Macaulay duality and its geometry Thanks: Both authors are deeply grateful to Tony Iarrobino for many discussions in person and in email about the content of this article and for providing many pertinent references, in particular about smoothable quotients and elementary components, which are discussed in ( 10.13 ). The second author also thanks Arvid Siqveland for discussions about deformations of modules. Finally, both authors are very grateful for the referee’s many thoughtful suggestions for improving the paper.
Steven L. Kleiman, Jan O. Kleppe

Abstract
. Macaulay Duality, between quotients of a polynomial ring over a field, annihilated by powers of the variables, and finitely generated submodules of the ring’s graded dual, is generalized over any Noetherian ring, and used to provide isomorphisms between the subschemes of the Hilbert scheme parameterizing various sorts of these quotients, and the corresponding subschemes of the Quot scheme of the dual. Thus notably the locus of recursively compressed algebras of permissible socle type is proved to be covered by open subschemes, each one isomorphic to an open subscheme of a certain affine space. Moreover, the polynomial variables are weighted, the polynomial ring is replaced by a graded module, and attention is paid to induced filtrations and gradings. Furthermore, a similar theory is developed for (relatively) maximal quotients of a graded Gorenstein Artinian algebra.



[name=notation,title=Notation,program=makeindex,options=-c,columns=4,columnsep=20pt]

[name=terminology,title=Terminology,columnsep=20pt,options=-c]

2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
13H10, 13C40, 14M05, 14C05

1.
Introduction

This introduction surveys previous work by many, explains our approach,
and summarizes our advances.

In 1972, Iarrobino
[
IM15
]
initiated the study of reducible
punctual Hilbert schemes. Since then, many researchers have carried on,
studying various loci of thick points, often with common support. Thus
certain subtle geometric issues arose, for which the present article
aims to give the first proper treatment in natural generality.

A key tool has been Macaulay duality. It provides a means to transform
loci in the local punctual Hilbert scheme into more tractable loci in a
related Quot scheme. However, previous treatments of Macaulay duality
are inadequate to the job, since (with a few limited exceptions) they’re
developed over a base field, and cannot properly handle flat families.
Thus in the present article, our first goal is to develop a suitable
version (
3.5
) of Macaulay duality over any Noetherian base
ring.

Our main goal stems from Iarrobino and Emsalem’s 1978 landmark paper

[
IE78
]
. They fixed a polynomial ring
P
in
r
variables over an
algebraically closed field
k
. Notably, they considered local
Gorenstein quotients
C
of
P
with Hilbert vector
(1,r,r,1)
. Using
Macaulay duality, they found that the homogeneous
C
fill an open
subset of the projective space on the cubic forms, and that over it, the
adically filtered
C
fill an affine-space bundle of fiber dimension

\binom{r}{2}
, whose structure map takes
C
to its associated graded
algebra.

Our main goal is to treat Iarrobino’s remarkable 1984 generalization in

[
Iar84
]
of the preceding results. The original
C
are generalized
by the
compressed

C
; these are the
C
whose length is maximal

among quotients that are annihilated by a power of each variable and
are of fixed “permissible” socle type. Iarrobino discovered and
Fröberg and Laksov reproved, see (
8.3
) below, that,
strikingly, the length of a compressed
C
can be computed a priori from
its socle type. This basic result grew out of
[
IE78
]
*Thm. 3.31,
p. 173; related work is discussed in the note at the bottom of p. 350
in
[
Iar84
]
. This result is assumed in the present paper. Rather,
we give the geometry a proper treatment.

We fix two flaws in
[
Iar84
]
, beyond the lack of use of a suitable
Macaulay duality. The first concerns the locus (in the Hilbert scheme)
of compressed homogeneous
C
. It is not true, as asserted in

[
Iar84
]
*Prop. 3.6, p. 356, that every
C
remains compressed
under recursion on the diameter of the socle type; see (
9.6
).
So to reach our main goal, we work with
recursively compressed

C
; these are the compressed
C
that do remain compressed under
recursion. It is their locus that is covered by open subschemes of
affine space; see (
9.3
)(2). However, we show in
(
9.10
) on the basis of the work of Iarrobino and of
Fröberg and Laksov mentioned above that a general
C
of socle type
bounded by
\mathbf{t}
is, in fact, recursively compressed of socle type

\mathbf{t}
.

The second flaw is the poor justification of the description in

[
Iar84
]
*Prop. 3.11, p. 359 of the retraction from the locus of
filtered
C
to that of homogeneous
C
. That justification proceeds by
induction on the minimal number of generators of the dual
C^{*}
.
However, adding new generators, one after the other, leads not to a
single quotient
C
, but to a flag of quotients. Therefore, we provide
a noninductive proof in (
10.10
).

The theory of compressed Artinian local rings
C
has been extended by
many authors in various directions. In his 1985 thesis

[
MirThesis
]
, Miri studied such
C
that are quotients of direct
sums of shifts
B:=\bigoplus_{q}P(q)^{\oplus\mathbf{t}(q)}
. However, in
his 1993 paper
[
MiriCA21
]
, which is based on
[
MirThesis
]
, he
took
B:=P^{\oplus t}
with
t\geq 1
. He focused on the
C
with
cyclic duals of maximal length of socle type
\mathbf{t}
. A stronger
version of his existence theorem
[
MiriCA21
]
*Prp. 2.8, p. 2846
with
B:=\bigoplus_{q}P(q)^{\oplus\mathbf{t}(q)}
is given in
(
9.2
)(1) below with a simpler proof. He also described the
varieties of graded
C
and of filtered
C
in

[
MiriCA21
]
*Thm. 3.1, p. 2853; his descriptions are special
cases of (
9.3
)(2) and (
10.12
).

In 2000, Boij studied graded level quotients
C
of
B:=P(s)^{\oplus t}
for
s,\,t\geq 1
. A version of of his existence theorem is given
for comparison with Miri’s in (
9.2
)(2).

In 2001, Cho and Iarrobino
[
C-IJA241
]
initiated the study of
(relatively) compressed quotients
C
of a proper quotient
A
of
P
;
these are the
C
whose length is maximal among quotients of fixed
“permissible” socle type. They gave examples of
A
where this
maximal length is not the a priori bound computed from the socle type and
the Hilbert function of
A
. A few years later, they and others gave
additional examples. There’s more discussion of this matter near the
end of the introduction.

In 2014, Rossi and Şega
[
RS-AM259
]
studied the Poincaré
series of finitely generated modules over compressed local Gorenstein

C
; they treated mixed characteristic
C
, a notable advance. In 2018,
Kustin, Şega, and Vraciu
[
KSV-JA505
]
extended that work to
compressed local
C
of arbitrary permissible socle type. Both papers
recall that such
C
exist over an infinite field, but otherwise, they
study such a
C
, assuming
C
exists. However, since a mixed
characteristic
C
isn’t flat over any base ring
k
, such a
C
isn’t
covered by our theory.

In our work,
P
is replaced by a finitely generated, graded algebra
A:=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}A_{n}
over an arbitrary Noetherian base ring
k
;
from Section 4 on,
A_{0}=k
. Further,
C
is now a quotient of a
finitely generated graded
A
-module
B=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}B_{n}
with

B_{n}
a locally free
k
-module of finite rank
\mathbf{b}(n)
. For example,

k
could be a field,
A
a
weighted
polynomial ring, and
B=A
; see (
5.4
) and (
5.5
), which illustrate an
advantage gained from a nonstandard weighting. However, unless
explicitly stated otherwise, a polynomi ring has the standard grading,
in which all the variables have weight 1. Furthermore, it may well be
useful to generalize our work to the case where
B
is filtered,
especially in the linkage results (
5.6
) and (
6.4
) when

C
is filtered, but not graded.

In general, the
C
of interest are locally free
k
-modules of finite
rank. Below, such a
C
is termed
k
-
Artinian
. Of course, if

k
isn’t Artinian, then neither is
C
. However,
C
can be viewed as
providing a flat family of Artinian quotients.

To treat the various loci
L
of Artinian quotients and the maps between
them, we use the rudiments of Grothendieck’s theory of representable
functors. The first job then is to take a
k
-scheme
T
of finite type
and to identify the
T
-points of
L
, namely, the
k
-maps
\tau\colon T\to L
. Below, each
L
of interest turns out to parameterize a universal
family
\mathscr{L}
of quotients. Then
\tau^{*}\mathscr{L}
is a family
parameterized by
T
; conversely, every family parameterized by
T
is
obtained via pullback along a unique
\tau
. Thus the
T
-points
\tau

are given by certain quotients
\mathscr{C}
on
T
. The first job is to
identify, a priori, these
\mathscr{C}
. Doing so means generalizing the
definition of the locus
L
in terms of
T
and
\mathscr{C}
. Here

T
-flatness plays a central role. Further, it suffices to use affine

T
. The second job is to give
L
a scheme structure and to construct
the universal family
\mathscr{L}
; in other words, the job is to represent
the functor of these
\mathscr{C}
.

To do the second job, we use Grothendieck’s theory of
flattening
stratification

(as it’s named in
[
CAS
]
*Lect. 8). The key
tool is Grothendieck’s Lem. 3.6 on p. 221-15 in
[
SB221
]
: given a
coherent sheaf
\mathscr{F}
on a locally Noetherian scheme
Q
and an integer

r
, there’s a (unique) subscheme
Z
of
Q
such that a map
\tau\colon T\to Q
factors through
Z
iff
\tau^{*}\mathscr{F}
is locally free of rank

r
. (To prove it, let
F_{r}
be the closed subscheme of
Q
defined by
the
(r-1)
st Fitting Ideal of
\mathscr{F}
, and set
Z=F_{r}-F_{r-1}
.)
Historically speaking, flattening stratification was introduced into the
study of loci of Artinian algebras by the second author in
[
PGor
]

and
[
MaxGor
]
.

For example, filter
B
by
F^{n}B:=\bigoplus_{p\geq n}B_{p}
, and consider
the locus of filtered,
k
-Artinian quotients of
B
with given Hilbert
function
\mathbf{h}
. What’s the value of the associated functor at
T:=\mathop{\rm Spec}(K)
with
K
a
k
-algebra? It’s the set of
A\otimes_{k}K
-quotients

C
of
B\otimes_{k}K
with the induced filtration such that
F^{n}C/F^{n+1}C

is locally free over
K
of rank
\mathbf{h}(n)
for all
n
. In
(
6.2
), we prove these sets form a functor.

Let’s represent this functor. Set
\ell:=\sum_{p}\mathbf{h}(p)
and
X:=\mathop{\rm Spec}(A)
. Denote the
\mathscr{O}_{X}
-module associated to
B
by
\widetilde{B}
.
Set
\smash{Q:=\mathop{\rm{\mathbb{Q}}uot}\nolimits_{\widetilde{B}/X/k}^{\ell}}
, and denote by
\mathscr{Q}
the
quotient of
B_{Q}
whose associated
\mathscr{O}_{X_{Q}}
-module is the universal
quotient of
\widetilde{B}_{Q}
. Given
q
, Grothendieck’s lemma yields a
subscheme
Q_{q}
of
Q
such that a map
\tau\colon T\to Q
factors through

Q_{q}
iff
\tau^{*}(\mathscr{Q}/F^{q}\mathscr{Q}):=(\mathscr{Q}/F^{q}\mathscr{Q})\otimes K
is
locally free of rank
\sum_{p<q}\mathbf{h}(p)
. Then the functor is
represented by
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}:=\bigcap_{q}Q_{q}
and the pullback

of
\mathscr{Q}
; see (
6.2
). Note it’s not enough to render all

\tau^{*}(F^{q}\mathscr{Q}/F^{q+1}\mathscr{Q})
flat, as possibly
\tau^{*}F^{q}\mathscr{Q}\neq F^{q}\tau^{*}\mathscr{Q}
, whereas
\tau^{*}(\mathscr{Q}/F^{q}\mathscr{Q})=\tau^{*}\mathscr{Q}\big/F^{q}\tau^{*}\mathscr{Q}
; a simple example was given by Granger

[
Granger
]
*p. 3, top.

Next, consider the sublocus of homogeneous quotients. Its subfunctor
has, as value at
T
, the subset of
C=\bigoplus_{p}C_{p}
with
C_{p}

locally free of rank
\mathbf{h}(p)
for all
p
. To represent it, set
Z:=\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Define
\mathscr{S}
by
\mathscr{Q}=:\widetilde{B}_{Q}/\mathscr{S}
.
Consider the pullback
\mathscr{Q}_{Z}
of
\mathscr{Q}
and its associated graded
quotient
G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Q}_{Z})
. Then
\mathscr{Q}_{Z}=\widetilde{B}_{Z}/\mathscr{S}_{Z}
, and

G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Q}_{Z})=\widetilde{B}_{Z}/G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{Z})
. Set
\mathscr{R}:=\widetilde{B}_{Z}/(\mathscr{S}_{Z}+G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{Z}))
. Grothendieck’s lemma yields a subscheme
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
of
Z
such that a map
T\to Z
factors through
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
iff
\mathscr{R}_{T}
is locally free of rank
\ell
; so iff
both
\mathscr{Q}_{T}\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{R}_{T}
and
G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Q}_{T})\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{R}_{T}
are
isomorphisms, see (
6.2
). Thus the subfunctor is represented by

\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
and the pullback of
\mathscr{Q}
.

Alternatively, form the maps
\beta_{n}\colon(\widetilde{B}_{Q})_{n}\to\mathscr{Q}
.
Grothendieck’s lemma yields a subscheme
Y_{n}
of
Q
such that a map

\tau\colon T\to Q
factors through
Y_{n}
iff
\tau^{*}\mathop{\rm Cok}(\beta_{n})
is
locally free of rank
\ell-\mathbf{h}(n)
. Then the subfunctor is
represented by
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}:=\bigcap_{n}Y_{n}
and the
pullback of
\mathscr{Q}
; see (
6.2
) again. Note it’s not enough to
render
\mathop{\rm Ker}(\beta_{n})
locally free, as forming a kernel does not always
commute with pullback, whereas forming a cokernel does.

The previous four paragraphs fix and generalize the second author’s 1998
proof of
[
PGor
]
*Thm. 1.1, p. 609 and his 2006 proof of

[
MaxGor
]
*Prp. 9, p. 3145. (Note that
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{h}
is
denoted by
{\rm GradAlg}(h)
in
[
PGor
]
and
[
MaxGor
]
.) The
particular case of homogeneous quotients also results from the 2004
general theory of Haiman and Sturmfels, see
[
HS04
]
*6.2, p. 766,
but both of our proofs above are simpler and more direct.

There are two natural maps: a closed embedding
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
and a retraction
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
. They represent maps of functors: the
inclusion of the homogeneous
C
into the filtered
C
, and the
retraction taking a filtered
C
to its associated graded quotient

G_{\bullet}C
. A priori,
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
is a monomorphism, but a posteriori, it’s a closed embedding, as the
schemes are separated and a retraction exists. See (
6.3
).

Jelisiejew kindly pointed out in email to the authors the following
interesting way to construct
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
and to study the retraction map
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
. This way involves the Białynicki-Birula
decomposition of the above Quot scheme
Q
under the natural action of
the multiplicative group. Namely,
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
appears as a
disjoint union of connected components of the decomposition, and
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
is its subscheme of fixed points. Consequently, the
retraction map is affine; in fact, near a point of
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
where the map is smooth, it’s an affine-space fibration.
Please see Prp. 3.1 and Rmk. 3.2 on p. 255 of
[
JJ-JLMS2019
]
,
and Thm. 1.1 and Prp. 5.3(3) and Sec. 7 of
[
JJ-LS
]
, and Sec. 5
of
[
JJ-KS
]
; note that in these papers,
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}

would be denoted by
\bigl(\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\bigr)^{+}
. These
papers place various restrictive hypotheses on
A
,
B
, and
S
;
however, Jelisiejew said he believes that, without change, the proofs
cover our setup. We do not pursue these ideas here, except indirectly
in (
10.13
) through references to
[
JJ-JLMS2019
]
.

To analyze the structure of
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
and of the
retraction
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
,
following
[
IE78
]
and
[
Iar84
]
, we generalize Macaulay duality.
Macaulay introduced it in 1916 in Chap. IV of
[
MacF
]
. In essence
(cf. Robert’s introduction in
[
MacF
]
), Macaulay fixed a power
series ring
\widehat{P}
over a field
k
, and formed the span,
P^{\dagger}
say,
of the Laurent monomials
1/M
for all monomials
M\in\widehat{P}
. He
viewed
P^{\dagger}
as an
\widehat{P}
-module: the product of a monomial
L\in\widehat{P}
with
1/M
is
L/M
if
L
divides
M
and is 0 if not. He called a

\psi\in\widehat{P}
and an
f\in P^{\dagger}
“inverse to one another” if

\psi\cdot f=0
, and he
[
MacF
]
* p. 68 defined the “inverse
system” of an ideal
I
containing powers of the variables to be the
set of all
f\in P^{\dagger}
inverse to all
\psi\in I
. Thus
P^{\dagger}

is the graded dual of the polynomial ring
P
, and the inverse system is

just
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{k}(P/(I\cap P),\,k)
. For instance, take
I:=(X_{1}X_{2},\,X_{1}^{2}-X_{2}^{3})
in
\widehat{P}:=k[[X_{1},X_{2}]]
; its inverse system in
P^{\dagger}:=k[X_{1}^{-1},X_{2}^{-1}]
is generated by a single element
f:=X_{1}^{-2}+X_{2}^{-3}
; see (
5.5
). The bijection between
I
, or
equivalently
\widehat{P}/I
, and its inverse system is commonly known as
“Macaulay Duality” (or “apolarity”). Our generalization is
(
3.5
).

Macaulay Duality is a special case of Matlis Duality, as introduced in
1958 in
[
MatDu
]
*Sec. 4, pp. 525–28 for an arbitrary complete
Noetherian local ring. Indeed,
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{k}(\widehat{P}/I,\,k)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\widehat{P}}(\widehat{P}/I,\,P^{\dagger})
, see the end of (
3.7
); hence,
P^{\dagger}
is the
injective hull of
k
by the uniqueness of dualizing modules. In 2018,
in
[
KES
]
*Sec 3, pp. 15–18; Sec. 5, pp. 20–22, Smith
developed a relative version of Matlis duality, for an arbitrary
Noetherian domain
k
and a Noetherian
k
-algebra
P
equipped with an
ideal
J
such that
P/J
is a finitely generated
k
-module. For any

P
-module
M
, set
M^{\vee}:=\varinjlim\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{k}(M/J^{n}M,\ k)
. Smith
proved
M^{\vee}=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{P}(M,\,P^{\vee})
, and when
P=k[[X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}]]

and
J=\langle X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}\rangle
, then
P^{\vee}=H_{J}^{n}(P)
. Notice

P^{\vee}=P^{\dagger}
.

Another version of relative Matlis Duality is given in (
3.3
).
In it,
P
is replaced by a
k
-Artinian
k
-algebra
A
where
k
is a
Noetherian ring. Set
A^{*}:=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{k}(A,k)
. This version asserts that

A^{*}
is a dualizing module for the
k
-Artinian
A
-modules.

In 1978, the first relative version of Macaulay Duality was stated,
without proof, by Emsalem
[
PEpais
]
*Prp. 18, p. 415 for a power
series ring over a local algebra of finite dimension over an
algebraically closed field
K
of characteristic 0 in order to study
deformations of local Artinian
K
-algebras.

In 1998, in Rmk. 1.9 on p. 613 of
[
PGor
]
, the second author
conjectured there should be a relative Macaulay duality yielding an
isomorphism of schemes
L\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\Gamma
where
\Gamma
is the open
subscheme of
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{P}^{\mathbf{h}}
of Gorenstein quotients of a
polynomial ring
P
over an algebraically closed field
k
of
characteristic not
2
and where
L
is the appropriate catalecticant
determinantal locus of
s
-forms, with
s:=\sup\{\,n\mid\mathbf{h}(n)\neq 0\,\}
; see (
6.8
) and (
6.9
). As evidence, he
constructed a canonical map of reductions
L_{\rm red}\to\Gamma_{\rm red}
; it’s bijective on
k
-points by Macaulay duality, and
corresponding
k
-points of
L
and
\Gamma
have the same tangent
space. In 2006, he elaborated on this construction in the proof of
Thm. 11 on p. 3146 in
[
MaxGor
]
, and in the following subsection,
Prb. 12, he noted that the arguments there prove that the map is a
topological isomorphism and so the dimensions at corresponding points
are equal. In 2007, on p. 694 in
[
LevAlg
]
, he extended this proof
to the case of level quotients. In particular, the equality of the
tangent spaces and of the dimensions yield a smoothness criterion for

L
.

In 2018 in
[
JJ-VSP
]
*Subsec. 2.2, pp. 271–78, Jelisiejew
developed a relative Macaulay Duality for a power series ring with
coefficients in a Noetherian algebra over an algebraically closed field

K
in order to study loci of Gorenstein Artinian
K
-algebras. The
Gorenstein hypothesis is weakened a bit in Sec. 4.4 of his 2017 thesis

[
JJthesis
]
. In the recent paper
[
JJ-KS
]
*Sec. 4.1, he and
Šivic developed a Macaulay Duality for a free module over a
polynomial ring with coefficients in an algebraically closed field.

Macaulay Duality was also generalized to higher-dimensional Gorenstein
quotients of the polynomial ring and the power series ring over a field
in 2017 by Elias and Rossi
[
ER-AM
]
. However, here we do not pursue
that direction.

Generalizing Macaulay Duality is our first goal. Our treatment is
independent of earlier work, and goes further in the way explained next;
yet it’s rather simple. For it, recall we fix a Noetherian ring
k
, a
finitely generated graded
k
-algebra
A:=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}A_{n}
, and a

k
-flat finitely generated graded
A
-module
B=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}B_{n}
.

Let
C
be a
k
-module. Set
C^{*}:=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{k}(C,k)
. If
C
is an

A
-module, then so is
C^{*}
via the adjoint action:
(af)\psi:=f(a\psi)
for all
a\in A
and
f\in C^{*}
and
\psi\in C
.

Assume
C
is locally free of finite rank. Then
C^{*}
is too, and the
natural map
C\to(C^{*})^{*}
is an isomorphism. In other words,
C\mapsto C^{*}
is a dualizing functor on these
C
. Further, see
(
3.2
), it induces a dualizing functor on the filtered
C
,
which restricts to a dualizing functor on the graded
C
, and which
respects the retraction that takes a filtered
C
to its associated
graded module.

Moreover, see (
3.4
) and (
3.5
), form the graded dual

B^{\dagger}:=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}(B_{n})^{*}
. Then the functor
C\mapsto C^{*}

yields a bijection from the
k
-Artinian quotients
C
of
B
onto the

k
-Artinian submodules
D
of
B^{\dagger}
with
B^{\dagger}/D
flat. Further,
this bijection respects the induced filtrations on
C
and
D
, and it
respects the retractions taking
C
and
D
to their associated graded
modules. To recast this bijection into the more traditional form of
apolarity, note that, if
C=B/I
, then
C^{*}=(0:_{B^{\dagger}}I)
, where

(0:_{B^{\dagger}}I)
is the apolar annihilator, which consists of all
f\in B^{\dagger}
with
\psi\cdot f=0
for all
\psi\in I
. Note
B^{\dagger\dagger}=B
; so
D^{*}=B/(0:_{B}D)
. Thus the bijection becomes
I\mapsto(0:_{B^{\dagger}}I)
with inverse
D\mapsto(0:_{B}D)
. That then is our
generalization of Macaulay Duality.

Our Macaulay Duality facilitates the study of the retraction
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
as follows.
Recall that, if
T:=\mathop{\rm Spec}(K)
with
K
a
k
-algebra, then a
T
-point
of
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
is given by a filtered
A\otimes_{k}K
-quotient

C
of
B\otimes_{k}K
with
F^{n}C/F^{n+1}C
locally free over
K
of rank
\mathbf{h}(n)
. Now, every map
K\to L
yields a natural isomorphism

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{K}(C,K)\otimes_{K}L\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{L}(C\otimes_{K}L,L)
. Thus Macaulay Duality
provides an isomorphism from the functor of points of
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
onto the functor whose value at
T
is the set of filtered
submodules
D
of
B\otimes_{k}K
with
F^{n}D/F^{n+1}D
locally free over
K

of rank
\mathbf{h}^{*}(n)
where
\mathbf{h}^{*}(n):=\mathbf{h}(-n)
and with

(B^{\dagger}\otimes_{k}K)/D
flat over
K
.

The latter functor is automatically representable by a copy of
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
, say
\mathbb{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
, and by
a subsheaf of the pullback of
B^{\dagger}
to
\mathbb{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
; this subsheaf is obtained from the universal quotient
on
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
by “Hom’ing” it into the structure
sheaf. Similarly, the subfunctor of homogeneous
D
is representable by
a copy of
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
, say
\mathbb{H}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
. Also,
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}

becomes the retraction
\mathbb{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{H}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
given by
D\mapsto G_{\bullet}D
.

Thus a locus of quotients with given Hilbert function
\mathbf{h}
can be
transformed into an isomorphic dual locus of submodules. Now, our main
goal is to treat, via this duality, the geometry of recursively
compressed quotients. Since they’re defined in terms of the socle type

\mathbf{t}
, the next step is to relativize and dualize this notion.

Assume
A/F^{1}A=k
. Fix a
k
-Artinian
A
-module
C
, set
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C):=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(k,\,C)
, and call this
A
-module the
k
-socle
of

C
. If
k
is a field, we recover the traditional notion. In general,

\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)=(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)^{*}
. A form of this equation was proved by
Iarrobino
[
Iar84
]
*Lem. 2.1, p. 346. It’s important, because

C^{*}\otimes_{A}k
is more useful than
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)
, as by Nakayama’s Lemma,

C^{*}\otimes_{A}k
locally governs the generators of
C^{*}
.
The equation holds, as both sides are equal to
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C^{*},\,k^{*})
;
the left, as
C\mapsto C^{*}
is a dualizing functor, and the right, by
Adjoint Associativity; see (
4.3
).

For instance, take
P:=k[X_{1},X_{2}]
and
C:=P/I
with
I:=(X_{1}X_{2},\,X_{1}^{2}-X_{2}^{3})
. Then, as is easy to see,
C^{*}
is generated
by
X_{2}^{-3}+X_{1}^{-2}
, and so
X_{2}^{-3}
and
X_{1}^{-1}
form a minimal
generating set of
G_{\bullet}(C^{*})
. Thus we obtain
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits(G_{\bullet}(C))=(X_{1},\,X_{2}^{3})
without even finding
G_{\bullet}(C)
; cf. (
5.5
).

Assume
C^{*}\otimes_{A}k
is
k
-Artinian. Then so is
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)
, and their
Hilbert functions, say
\mathbf{t}^{*}
and
\mathbf{t}
, satisfy
\mathbf{t}^{*}(p)=\mathbf{t}(-p)
, as
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)=(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)^{*}
. Extending tradition, we call

\mathbf{t}
the
k
-socle type
of
C
, and
\mathbf{t}^{*}
the
local

generator type
of
C^{*}
. Notice that, given any maximal ideal of

k
, its complement contains an element
f
such that the localization

C^{*}_{f}
is generated by a set with
\mathbf{t}^{*}(p)
elements of degree
p

for all
p
.

For every
k
-algebra
K
, the
A\otimes_{k}K
-module
(C\otimes_{k}K)^{*}
is also of
local generator type
\mathbf{t}^{*}
as
(C\otimes_{k}K)^{*}=C^{*}\otimes_{k}K
; so

C\otimes_{k}K
is of
K
-socle type
\mathbf{t}
. Set
T:=\mathop{\rm Spec}(K)
. It
follows that the subsets of the
T
-points of
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}

and
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
given by those quotients of socle type
\mathbf{t}
form functors in
T
, which are compatible with retraction.
Similarly, the subsets of the
T
-points of
\mathbb{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
given by those submodules of
local generator type
\mathbf{t}^{*}
form functors in
T
, which are
compatible with retraction.

The former functors are represented by subschemes of
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
, say
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
. Indeed, flattening

stratification yields
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
much as above for

\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
, and we can take
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}:=\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}\cap\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
; see
(
6.7
). Now, Macaulay Duality gives isomorphisms from the
former functors to the latter. So they’re represented by copies of
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
, say
\mathbb{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*},\mathbf{t}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*},\mathbf{t}}
,

which are subschemes of
\mathbb{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
and

\mathbb{H}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
. Also,
\mathbb{H}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*},\mathbf{t}}=\mathbb{H}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}\cap\mathbb{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*},\mathbf{t}}
. Now, for
\mathbf{h}
fixed, as
\mathbf{t}
varies, the
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}

stratify
(that is, disjointly cover)
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
and

\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
; see (
6.7
). So by Macaulay Duality,
the
\mathbb{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*},\mathbf{t}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*},\mathbf{t}}
stratify
\mathbb{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
and

\mathbb{H}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
.

From Section 7 on, many arguments proceed via recursion on the
diameter

(s-\bar{s})
of
\mathbf{t}
, where
s:=\sup\{\,p\mid\mathbf{t}(p)>0\,\}
and
\bar{s}:=\inf\,\{\,p\mid\mathbf{t}(p)\neq 0\,\}
. Note
that, if
C
has Hilbert function
\mathbf{h}
, plainly
s:=\sup\{\,p\mid\mathbf{h}(p)>0\,\}
; also,
\mathbf{t}(s):=\mathbf{h}(s)
by (
4.4
)(2).

The recursion exits when
\bar{s}=s
, or equivalently,
\mathbf{t}(p):=0

for
p\neq s
. Then we call
\mathbf{t}
and
C

level
, and set

\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}:=\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
and

\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}:=\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
.

In (
6.7
), there’s an alternative construction of
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
; it shows
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
is an open
subscheme of
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
. So
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}

is an open subscheme of
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Sometimes,
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}-\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
contains
k
-points; an
example is the quotient
D^{*}
in (
9.7
). Also, the
retraction
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\to\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
needn’t
carry
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
into
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
;
indeed, (
5.5
) discusses a (standard) example of a
(
k
-Gorenstein) level filtered
C
with
G_{\bullet}C
not level. However,
by (
4.7
), if
G_{\bullet}C
is level, then so is
C
; thus the
preimage of
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
lies in
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Jelisiejew develops some of the theory of
k
-Gorenstein modules
for arbitrary
k
in
[
JJ-VSP
]
, especially in Subsection 2.2.

There’s a third construction of
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
studied in
(
6.8
)–(
6.11
). This time,
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}

arises, via flattening stratification, as a subscheme of the
Grassmannian
\mathbb{G}
of rank-
\mathbf{t}(s)
locally free quotients of
B_{s}
. Moreover, for
\mathbf{t}
fixed, as
\mathbf{h}
varies, the
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
stratify
\mathbb{G}
. Hence, if
k
is a domain,
then
\mathbb{G}
is reduced and irreducible, and its generic point lies in
some
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
. This
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
is
reduced and irreducible, and it’s covered by open subschemes, each one
isomorphic to an open subscheme of the affine space over
k
of fiber
dimension
t(\mathbf{b}(s)-t)
where
t:=\mathbf{h}(s)=\mathbf{t}(s)
and
\mathbf{b}

is the Hilbert function of

B
. Also, by lower semicontinuity of rank, if
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}^{\diamond}}\neq\emptyset
, then
\mathbf{h}^{\diamond}(p)\leq\mathbf{h}(p)
for all
p
; so

\sum_{p}\mathbf{h}^{\diamond}(p)\leq\sum_{p}\mathbf{h}(p)
, with equality iff
\mathbf{h}^{\diamond}=\mathbf{h}
. This third construction yields the isomorphism
L\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\Gamma

conjectured in Rmk. 1.9 on p. 613 of
[
PGor
]
and discussed above.

For a moment, suppose
B=A
. Then
C
is a
k
-algebra, and
C^{*}
is,
by (
3.3
), its dualizing module. Moreover,
C^{*}
is invertible
iff
C
is level and
\mathbf{t}(s)=1
. If so, we say
C
is
k
-Gorenstein
. (Likely, Grothendieck coined the term “Gorenstein”,

and Serre in 1960 was the first to publish it, but historically it’s
more correct to honor any one of several others, starting with Macaulay;
see
[
Hun
]
*pp. 56, 61–62 and
[
Eis
]
*Sec. 21.2,
pp. 529–30.) The condition for
C
to be
k
-Gorenstein doesn’t
depend on its filtration, but that for
G_{\bullet}C
does; see (
5.5
)
for an example. If
B
is
k
-Artinian and
k
-Gorenstein, but
C
just

k
-Artinian, then the linked ideal
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,A)\subset A
and the
Macaulay dual
C^{*}
of
C
coincide up to tensor product with a certain
invertible
k
-module; see (
5.6
).

In general, when
\bar{s}<s
, the recursion proceeds by replacing a
given homogeneous
k
-Artinian
C
by the unique graded quotient
C^{\prime}

with
socle diameter

s-\bar{s}-1
, provided
C^{\prime}
is
k
-flat,
and

when
\bar{s}+1<s
, similarly replaceable. Thus we are led to form
the sequence of quotients
\Lambda^{m}C
of
C
where
(\Lambda^{m}C)^{*}
is
the submodule of
C^{*}
generated by all the homogeneous elements of
degree at most
-m
. So
C^{\prime}=\Lambda^{\bar{s}+1}C
. Furthermore, every

\Lambda^{m}C
must be
k
-flat; when so, we call
C

multilevel
,

and denote the Hilbert function of
\Lambda^{m}C
by
\mathbf{h}_{m}
. Note that

\mathbf{h}_{\bar{s}}
is the Hilbert function of
C
.

Of course, when
k
is a field, flatness is automatic, and so every
C
is
multilevel.

Then all the
(\Lambda^{m}C)^{*}
are flat too by (
4.14
)(3). Note

\Lambda^{m}\Lambda^{n}C=\Lambda^{n}C
for
m\leq n
and

\Lambda^{m}\Lambda^{n}C=\Lambda^{m}C
for
m\geq n
for any
n
. Take
n:=\bar{s}+1
. Thus
C^{\prime}
is multilevel of socle type
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
. Also,
set
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{m}:=\mathbf{h}_{\bar{s}+1}
for
m\leq\bar{s}+1
and
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{m}:=\mathbf{h}_{m}
for
m\geq\bar{s}+1
. Then
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{m}
is the Hilbert function
of
\Lambda^{m}C^{\prime}
. We call
\{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{m}\}
the
attendant
to

\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}
. As to a filtered module
C
, we call
C

multilevel
if
G_{\bullet}C
is multilevel.

The algebraic theory of multilevel modules is treated in
(
4.13
)–(
4.15
), and the geometric theory, in
Secs. 7–10. Most of the effort concerns graded modules. For example,
in (
7.2
), we prove that, as
\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}
varies with
\mathbf{h}_{0}
fixed, the locus of all graded and all filtered multilevel
quotients of
B
are parameterized by (possibly empty) subschemes
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}
and
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}
of

\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}_{0}}
and
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}_{0}}
, stratifying

them. We use flattening stratification to construct
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}
, and we take
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}
to be the preimage of
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}
under the
retraction
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}_{0}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}_{0}}
.

A recursive construction of the
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}
is
given in (
7.3
)–(
7.5
). It’s harder, but leads to
three new notions, treated in (
7.6
)–(
7.10
). First, we
call
\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}

recursively maximal
for
\mathbf{t}
and
T/k
if

(1) when
\bar{s}<s
, the attendant
\{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{m}\}
is recursively
maximal for the attendant
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
; and (2) the
\mathbf{h}_{m}
termwise bound
the Hilbert functions of any multilevel quotient
C^{\diamond}
on any

T^{\diamond}/T
of
T^{\diamond}
-socle type
\mathbf{t}
, provided
\Lambda^{\bar{s}+1}\mathscr{C}^{\diamond}
has the
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{m}
as its Hilbert functions when
\bar{s}<s
;
and (3) there’s some multilevel quotient
C^{\sharp}
on some
T^{\sharp}/T
with
socle type
\mathbf{t}
and Hilbert functions the
\mathbf{h}_{m}
.

Second, we call
\mathbf{t}

quasi-permissible
if
\bar{s}=s
and

\mathbf{b}(s)\geq\mathbf{t}(s)
, or if
\bar{s}<s
and (1) the attendant
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{t}
is quasi-permissible, and (2) given any
T^{\flat}/S
and

\{\mathbf{h}^{\flat}_{m}\}
recursively maximal for
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
and
T^{\flat}
,
necessarily
\mathbf{b}(\bar{s})-\mathbf{h}^{\flat}_{\bar{s}+1}(\bar{s})\geq\mathbf{t}(\bar{s})
. Third, given a multilevel quotient
C
on some
T/k
, we call
C

recursively compressed
for
\mathbf{t}
if
C
is of
T
-socle type

\mathbf{t}
; if
\Lambda^{\bar{s}+1}C
is recursively compressed for
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}

when
\bar{s}<s
; and if, for any multilevel quotient
C^{\diamond}
on any

T^{\diamond}/S
of
T^{\diamond}
-socle type
\mathbf{t}
, with
\Lambda^{\bar{s}+1}C^{\diamond}

recursively compressed for
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
if
\bar{s}<s
, necessarily
\mathop{\rm rank}(C^{\diamond})\leq\mathop{\rm rank}(C)
.

Those three notions have these properties. If a recursively maximal
sequence for
\mathbf{t}
and
T/k
exists, then it’s unique and
\mathbf{t}
is
quasi-permissible. If
\mathbf{t}
is quasi-permissible and
k
is a domain,
then a recursively maximal sequence
\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}
for
\mathbf{t}
and
T/k

exists; moreover,
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}
is nonempty, reduced,
irreducible, and covered by open subschemes, with each one isomorphic to
an open subscheme of the affine space over
k
of fiber dimension
N

where
\mathbf{N}:=\sum_{p}\mathbf{t}(p)\bigl(\mathbf{b}(p)-\mathbf{h}_{\bar{s}}(p)\bigr)
.
Every recursively compressed
C
for
\mathbf{t}
is of the same rank. If

\mathbf{t}
is quasi-permissible, then (1) there’s a recursively compressed

C
for
\mathbf{t}
on some
T/k
, and (2) given a recursively maximal
sequence
\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}
for
\mathbf{t}
and some
T/k
, and given on some

T^{\sharp}
a
C^{\sharp}
of socle type
\mathbf{t}
, each
\mathbf{h}_{m}
is the Hilbert
function of
\Lambda^{m}C^{\sharp}
iff
\mathscr{C}^{\sharp}
is recursively compressed
for
\mathbf{t}
.

Those three notions are particularly important when
k
is an infinite
field,
A
is a polynomial ring in two or more variables, and
B=A
.
This fact was discovered by Iarrobino, and treated in
[
Iar84
]
. We
abstract, clarify and extend his work from (
8.1
) on. Our main
results — (
8.8
), (
9.3
), (
10.10
),
(
10.12
) — extend his main results  — Thms. IIA, IIB,
IIC on p. 351 in
[
Iar84
]
. His results describe the geometry of

\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{A}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}
, of
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{A}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}
, and
of the retraction
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{A}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{A}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}
when
\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}
is recursively maximal for
\mathbf{t}
and
\mathbf{t}
is permissible; these issues are discussed next. In particular, in
our extensions,
k
can be any Noetherian base ring, and
A=\bigoplus_{p}A_{p}
, any finitely generated, graded
k
-algebra with
A_{p}

a locally free
k
-module of rank, say,
\mathbf{a}(p)
.

In general, set
g_{m}(p):=\textstyle\sum_{q=m}^{s}\mathbf{t}(q)\,\mathbf{a}(q-p)
and

\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}(p):=\min\{\,\mathbf{g}_{m}(p),\,\mathbf{b}(p)\,\}
and
\beta^{\rm I}_{m}:=\textstyle\sum_{p}\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}(p)
for all
m
,
p
;

see (
8.1
); the I’s honor Iarrobino’s work. Then given any
m

and any homogeneous multilevel quotient
C
of
B
of socle type
\mathbf{t}
, the Hilbert function
\mathbf{h}_{m}
of
\Lambda^{m}C
satisfies
\mathbf{h}_{m}(p)\leq\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}(p)
for all
p
, and also
\mathop{\rm rank}(\Lambda^{m}C)\leq\beta^{\rm I}_{m}
, with equality iff
\mathbf{h}_{m}=\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}
, as

C^{*}
is of local generator type
\mathbf{t}^{*}
; see (
8.2
).

We call the
C
above I-
compressed
if
\mathbf{h}_{m}=\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}
for all
m
, that is, if
C
is represented by a point of
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}}
; in addition,
\mathbf{t}
must be
permissible, where
permissible
means that
\mathbf{t}(p)=0
if
\mathbf{b}(p)<\mathbf{g}_{\bar{s}}(p)
and if (
8.3
)(d) holds; the latter
holds, for instance, when
\mathbf{a}(p)
and
\mathbf{b}(p)
are nondecreasing in

p
. In (
8.3
), the meaning of “permissible”

is extended to rather arbitrary
A
and
B
.

For instance, let
k
be a field, and
B:=A:=k[X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}]
the
polynomial ring (with the standard grading). Let the socle vector be

(0,0,0,1,2)
; that is, the nonzero values of
\mathbf{t}
are
\mathbf{t}(q)=1

if
q=3
and
\mathbf{t}(q)=2
if
q=4
. Then
s=4
and
{\bar{s}}=3
; further, for
m\geq 3
the nonzero
g_{m}
are
g_{4}(p)=2\mathbf{a}(4-p)

and
g_{3}(p)=g_{4}(p)+\mathbf{a}(3-p)
. So
\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{4}(p)=g_{4}(p)
and

\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{3}(p)=g_{3}(p)
for
p\geq 3
; further, the coordinates of
the vectors
(1,3,6,6,2)
and
(1,3,6,7,2)
given by
\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{4}

and
\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{3}
are at least those of the Hilbert vectors of

\Delta^{4}C
and
C
. The latter are of dimensions at most 18 and 19.
Thus any
C
of socle type
\mathbf{t}
and dimension 19 has Hilbert vector

(1,3,6,7,2)
, and if
\Delta^{4}C
is 18-dimensional too, then
C
is
I-compressed and recursively compressed.

Iarrobino proved (and Fröberg and Laksov reproved) in essence that,
remarkably, there exists an I-compressed
C
, or a
k
-point of
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{A}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}}
, if
\mathbf{t}
is permissible,
k
is an
infinite field,
A
is a polynomial ring, and
B=A
(Iarrobino also
assumed
\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{\bar{s}}(1)=\mathbf{a}(1)
, but this assumption is
easily removed); see (
8.3
). Consequently, there’s such a
C

if
k
is replaced by any (Noetherian) local ring with an infinite
residue field; see (
9.4
). Furthermore, see (
8.8
),
then
\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}
is recursively maximal for
\mathbf{t}
.
Moreover, a permissible
\mathbf{t}
is, by (
8.4
)(4),
quasi-permissible.

Preserve Iarrobino’s setup. So his existence theorem holds. It
follows, see (
8.8
), that a homogeneous quotient
C
of
A
of
socle type
\mathbf{t}
is I-compressed iff it’s recursively compressed;.
Also,
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{A}^{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{\bar{s}},\mathbf{t}}
is
irreducible, and
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{A}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}}
is open in it;
see (
9.9
).

Further, in essence, Iarrobino, just after Prop. 3.4 of

[
Iar84
]
*p. 339, constructed an irreducible scheme
P
whose

k
-points represent, with repetition, all the homogeneous
C
of socle
type bounded (termwise) by
\mathbf{t}
, and he asserted
P
has a nonempty
open subset whose
k
-points represent most compressed
C
of socle type

\mathbf{t}
. There’s a more refined assertion in (
9.10
): all
the compressed
C
of socle type
\mathbf{t}
correspond to an open subset of

P
, and those recursively compressed, to a nonempty, smaller open
subset. To put it more informally, a general
C
of socle type
\mathbf{t}

is recusively compressed.

Conversely, when
k
is any Noetherian ring,
A
is a polynomial ring,
and
B=A
, if there exists a
C
with
\mathop{\rm rank}(\Lambda^{m}C)=\beta^{\rm I}_{m}
for all
m
, then
\mathbf{t}
is permissible by (
8.6
).
On the other hand, the literature contains many interesting examples
with
k
a field,
A
a proper quotient of a polynomial ring,
B=A
,
and
\mathbf{t}
permissible; sometimes there are no
C
with

\mathop{\rm rank}(\Lambda^{m}C)=\beta^{\rm I}_{m}
for all
m
, and sometimes, there
are some.

Published examples of the latter case are reviewed in (
8.12
).
There all the
C
are Gorenstein; so the rank condition reduces to
\dim C=\beta^{\rm I}_{s}
. Moreover, suppose
A
too is Artinian and
Gorenstein. Then
\dim C=\beta^{\rm I}_{s}
when both
A
and
C
are
suitably general for their socle degrees; see (
8.11
). On the
other hand, (
8.10
) constructs examples where the Hilbert
function
\mathbf{h}
of
C
has a nontrivial “plateau”; the construction is
largely distilled and suitably adapted from
[
LNM1721
]
*pp. 9–14.

When, as above,
\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}
is recursively maximal for a
permissible
\mathbf{t}
, more can be proved via careful work with the spaces
of local generators of
C^{*}
. First, by (
9.3
), whether or not

k
is a domain,
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}}
is an open
subscheme of
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{\bar{s}}}
, and it’s
covered by open subschemes, with each one isomorphic to an open
subscheme of the affine space over
k
of fiber dimension
\mathbf{H}:=\sum_{p}\mathbf{t}(p)\bigl(\mathbf{b}(p)-\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{\bar{s}}(p)\bigr)
.
Second, if
A
and
B
satisfy certain mild conditions, which hold when

A
is a polynomial ring and
B=A
, then by (
10.10
) the
retraction map makes
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{\bar{s}},\mathbf{t}}

an affine-space bundle over
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{\bar{s}},\mathbf{t}}
of fiber dimension
\mathbf{R}:=\sum_{p}\mathbf{t}(p)\big(\sum_{q<p}(\mathbf{b}(q)-\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{\bar{s}}(q))\bigr)
. Finally, by (
10.12
),
which is a corollary of (
9.3
) and (
10.10
), then
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{\mathscr{B}}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}}
is covered by open subschemes,
each one isomorphic to an open subscheme of the affine space over
k
of
fiber dimension
\mathbf{F}:=\mathbf{H}+\mathbf{R}=\sum_{p}\mathbf{t}(p)\big(\sum_{q\leq p}(\mathbf{b}(q)-\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{\bar{s}}(q))\bigr)
; moreover, if
S
is
irreducible, then so are
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{\mathscr{B}}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}}

and
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{\mathscr{B}}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}}
.

An interesting issue, raised by the referee, is to determine when the
retraction map is an algebraic vector bundle. The referee noted that
Iarrobino
[
IaTop
]
*Thm. 1, p. 229; Thm. 2, p. 232 proved in
two variables that it’s not for the (nonmaximal) Hilbert vector

(1,1,1,\dotsc,1)
of length
n\geq 4
and that his result was
generalized by Haboush and Hyeon
[
HaHy
]
*Thm. 9.2, p. 4309 to

r
variables. On the other hand, Jelisiejew proved in

[
JJ-VSP
]
*Claim 2, p. 280 that it is a vector bundle in six
variables for the Hilbert vector
(1,6,6,1)
.

The formula for
\mathbf{F}
and the smoothness and irreducibility of

\smash{\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{\mathscr{B}}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}}}
are useful in the
study of
d
-dimensional quotients
C
of the polynomial ring
A
in
r

variables over a field
k
, particularly in showing that certain
C

aren’t smoothable within
\mathop{\rm Hilb}^{d}_{\mathbb{A}^{r}/k}
and in finding
elementary components
of
\mathop{\rm Hilb}^{d}_{\mathbb{A}_{k}^{r}/k}
(ones parameterizing

C
with absolutely irreducible support).
Examples are discussed in (
10.13
). Notably, there’s a new
example of a
small elementary component
(one of dimension less
than
dr
) with
k=\mathbb{Q}
and with Hilbert vector

(1,5,6,1)
. It’s produced via a novel method of reduction to the case

k=\mathbb{Z}/p
for just one
p>0
, see (
10.14
), accompanied by
computations over
\mathbb{Z}/p
with Macaulay 2.

The study of elementary components is generally based on the method of
small tangent spaces or trivial negative tangents, introduced in 1978 by
Iarrobino and Emsalem
[
IE78
]
and put in definitive form in 2019 by
Jelisiejew
[
JJ-JLMS2019
]
. Versions of Jelisiejew’s results,
adequate for the examples in (
10.13
), are, in (
10.14
),
given self-contained alternative proofs, largely inspired by
Jelisiejew’s.

Examples abound of proper quotients
A
of a polynomial ring
over a field
k
and of permissible-like
\mathbf{t}
such that every
homogeneous quotient
C
of
A
of
k
-socle type
\mathbf{t}
has
\dim_{k}C<\beta^{\rm I}_{\bar{s}}
; in particular, no
C
has
\mathop{\rm rank}(\Lambda^{m}C)=\beta^{\rm I}_{m}
for all
m
, and
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{A}^{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{\bar{s}},\mathbf{t}}=\emptyset
. Some examples were given by Cho and Iarrobino
in
[
C-IJA241
]
*Prp. 2.7, p. 754, and were analyzed by Geramita
et al. in
[
GerMAM186
]
*Ex. 7.3, pp. 70–72. The present second
author made an infinitesimal analysis of many classes of examples of
this sort; it suggests the geometry of
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{A}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}

and
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{A}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
too is essentially as above.

More examples were given by Iarrobino
[
Pen2005
]
*Ex. 2.9,
p. 283, by Zanello
[
ZanCA35
]
*Rmk. 6, p. 1090, and by
Migliore, Miró-Roig, and Nagel in
[
MMN
]
*Exs. 2.14–2.16,
pp. 343–344. The last ones are discussed in some detail and refined
in (
11.8
), as they nicely illustrate (
11.7
), which is
the main result of Section
11
.

Section 11 develops a theory like that in Sections 9 and 10, but now for
a
k
-Gorenstein,
k
-Artinian
A
over any Noetherian base
k
. The
maximal Hilbert function of a quotient
C
is often not
\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{\bar{s}}
, but is still a predictable function
\mathbf{h}
. The main
results (
11.7
)(1a)–(1c) are geometric: if
\mathbf{t}
vanishes
when it should and if
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{A}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}\neq\emptyset
,
then
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{A}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
is covered by nonempty open
subschemes, each one isomorphic to an open subscheme of the affine space
over
S
of fiber dimension
\mathbf{H}:=\sum_{p}\mathbf{t}(p)\bigl(\mathbf{a}(p)-\mathbf{h}(p)\bigr)
; further,
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{A}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
is an
affine-space bundle over
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{A}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
of fiber
dimension
\mathbf{R}:=\sum_{p}\mathbf{t}(p)\bigl(\sum_{p^{\prime}<p}(\mathbf{a}(p^{\prime})-\mathbf{h}(p^{\prime}))\bigr)
; finally,
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{A}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
is covered by
nonempty open subschemes, each one isomorphic to an open subscheme of
the affine space over
S
of fiber dimension
\mathbf{F}:=\mathbf{H}+\mathbf{R}=\sum_{p}\mathbf{t}(p)\big(\sum_{p^{\prime}\leq p}(\mathbf{a}(p^{\prime})-\mathbf{h}(p^{\prime}))\bigr)
.

The key to Section 11 is further work with local generators of
C^{*}
for
a given
C\in\mathbb{H}\Psi_{A}^{\mathbf{h}^{\diamond},\mathbf{t}}
for some
\mathbf{h}^{\diamond}
. Here’s the idea. Set
E:=\bigoplus_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}A(q)^{\oplus\mathbf{t}(q)}
. Now,
C^{*}
is of local generator type
\mathbf{t}^{*}
. So given any
maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of k, we may replace
k
by the localization
k_{h}

for an
h\notin\mathfrak{m}
so that
C^{*}
has a minimal set of homogeneous
generators
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{m}
, which define a surjection
E\twoheadrightarrow C^{*}
of
degree
0
. Then
\mathbf{h}^{\diamond}=\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{\bar{s}}
iff there’s
n<0
with
E_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}(C^{*})_{p}
for
p<n
and
(C^{*})_{p}=A^{\dagger}_{p}
for

p\geq n
. To proceed, for
p<n
, we let the kernel of
E_{p}\twoheadrightarrow(C^{*})_{p}
be nonzero in a controlled, but general, fashion.

Say
A^{\dagger}=Af
with
f\in A^{\dagger}_{-a}
. So
f_{i}=g_{i}f
for some

g_{i}\in A_{d_{i}}
with
d_{i}>0
. Then
\mathbf{h}^{\diamond}=\mathbf{h}
iff the Koszul
homology group
H_{1}(g_{1},\dotsc,g_{m};A^{\dagger})
is
0
in degrees below
n
;
see (
11.3
). If so and if
k_{red}
is a domain, then

g_{1},\dotsc,g_{m}
are general; see (
11.4
). When
k
is an
infinite field and
A
is a general complete intersection, Fröberg’s
conjecture asserts that, if
g_{1},\dotsc,g_{m}
are general, then indeed

\mathbf{h}^{\diamond}=\mathbf{h}
; see (
11.9
).

In brief, Section 2 treats preliminaries about the category
\mathbb{F}_{A}
of

filtered modules over a filtered ring
A
. However, (
2.4
) makes
a novel observation: the Rees functor embeds
\mathbb{F}_{A}
into the Abelian
category of graded modules over the Rees algebra of
A
as an exact
subcategory in Quillen’s sense
[
Q
]
*p. 99. Section 3 develops
Macaulay Duality over any Noetherian base ring
k
, providing the tool
used to transform loci of quotients into loci of submodules. Section 4
discusses a key invariant, the
k
-socle type
\mathbf{t}
of an
A
-module

C
. Its importance stems from (
4.3
): if
C
is
k
-Artinian
, that is, locally free of finite rank over
k
, then the
“reverse”
\mathbf{t}^{*}
of
\mathbf{t}
is the local generator type of the dual

C^{*}
. Section 5 discusses
k
-Gorenstein

k
-Artinian
A
; those

are the
A
with
A^{*}
an invertible
A
-module. Most of the results in
Section 5 are familiar when
k
is a field, but these
A
are used in
some interesting new examples.

From Section 6 on, the focus is geometry. Section 6 constructs various
schemes of quotients of a fixed graded
A
-module
B
when
A
is a
graded
k
-algebra with
A_{0}=k
. Notably, there are several different
constructions of the schemes
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
of filtered and of homogeneous, level quotients with
Hilbert function
\mathbf{h}
. Section 7 constructs the schemes
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}}
of
multilevel quotients, and treats three new notions: recursively maximal

\{\mathbf{h}_{m}\}
, quasi-permissible
\mathbf{t}
, and recursively compressed
multilevel
C
. Section 8 treats, in greater generality, Iarrobino’s
notions of a permissible
\mathbf{t}
and of the I-set (our term)
\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}
of any
\mathbf{t}
. Sections 9 and 10 extend Iarrobino’s
main results in
[
Iar84
]
, which describe the geometry of
\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{A}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}}
, of
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{A}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}}
, and of the retraction
\mathbb{F}\Lambda_{A}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{H}\Lambda_{A}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\rm I}_{m}\}}
when
\mathbf{t}
is
permissible and
A
is a polynomial ring, thus achieving our main goal.
Finally, Section 11 develops a similar theory for maximal quotients of a
graded Gorenstein Artinian algebra.

2.
Preliminaries

(2.1) Setup.

Fix a Noetherian base ring
k
for use throughout this paper. (In this
paper, all rings are commutative with 1.)

Recall for frequent, but implicit, use that (even when
k
is not
Noetherian) the following three conditions on a
k
-module
C
are
equivalent:

(1)

C
is locally free of finite (but not necessarily constant) rank
over
k
;

(2)

C
is projective and finitely generated over
k
;

(3)

C
is flat and finitely presented over
k
.

Given a
k
-algebra
A
, denote the (Abelian) category of
A
-modules
by
\mathbb{M}_{A}
.

Given any
k
-module
C
, define its
k
-dual

C^{*}
by

C^{*}:=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{k}(C,\,k).

If
C
is a module over a given
k
-algebra
A
, then
C^{*}
is
canonically an
A
-module too, via the
adjoint action
:
(af)\psi:=f(a\psi)
for all
a\in A
and
f\in C^{*}
and
\psi\in C
.

A map of
A
-modules
\gamma\colon C\to D
induces its
dual map
of

A
-modules
\gamma^{*}\colon D^{*}\to C^{*}
by
\gamma^{*}g:=g\circ\gamma
. And

C\mapsto C^{*}
defines a left exact
A
-linear functor
*\colon\mathbb{M}_{A}\to\mathbb{M}_{A}
.

(2.2)  (Filtered modules).

Fix a
k
-algebra
A
with a
(descending) filtration

F^{\bullet}A
;

namely,
F^{\bullet}A
is a descending chain of
A
-submodules
F^{m}A
for

m\in\mathbb{Z}
with
F^{m}A=A
for
m\leq 0
and with
F^{m}A\cdot F^{n}A\subset F^{m+n}A
for all
m
and
n
.

Given an
A
-module
C
, by a
filtration

F^{\bullet}C
, let us mean a
descending chain of
A
-submodules
F^{n}C
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
with
F^{m}A\cdot F^{n}C\subset F^{m+n}C
for all
m
and
n
. Set

\textstyle G_{n}C:=F^{n}C/F^{n+1}C\kern 5.0pt\text{for all }nG_{\bullet}C:=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}G_{n}C.

Then
G_{m}A\cdot G_{n}A\subset G_{m+n}A
and
G_{m}A\cdot G_{n}C\subset G_{m+n}C
for all
m
and
n
. Also,
G_{n}A=0
for
n<0
. Thus
G_{\bullet}A
is a graded
k
-algebra,
G_{\bullet}C
a graded
G_{\bullet}A
-module. As
usual, call them the
associated graded
algebra and module.

Notice that
A
always has a
trivial filtration
, where
F^{m}A:=A

for
m\leq 0
and
F^{m}A:=0
for
m>0
. Then
G_{0}A=A
and
G_{n}A=0
for
n\neq 0
. Similarly,
C
has a trivial filtration. Thus it’s
really no restriction to assume
A
and
C
are filtered.

Given
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
C[p]
denote
C
with its
shifted
filtration
,
F^{n}(C[p]):=F^{n+p}C
.

Call
F^{\bullet}C

exhaustive
if
\,\bigcup_{n}F^{n}C=C
,
separated
if
\,\bigcap_{n}F^{n}C=0
,
discrete
if
F^{n}C=0
for

n\gg 0
, and
finite
if
F^{n}C=C
for
n\ll 0
and
F^{n}C=0
for

n\gg 0
.

The filtered
A
-modules form a category
\mathbb{F}_{A}
. Its maps
\beta\colon(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)\to(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)
are the
A
-maps
\beta\colon B\to C
with

\beta(F^{n}B)\subset F^{n}C
for all
n
.

Denote by
\mathbb{EF}_{A}
the full subcategory of
\mathbb{F}_{A}
of all

(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)
with
F^{\bullet}B
exhaustive, and by
\mathbb{DEF}_{A}
its full
subcategory of all
(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)
with
F^{\bullet}B
discrete too.

Given
(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)
and
(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)
in
\mathbb{F}_{A}
, filter the

A
-module
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(B,\,C)
by

\displaystyle F^{p}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(B,\,C)

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}\bigl(B,\,C[p]\,\bigr)

\displaystyle:=\{\,\beta\colon B\to C\mid\beta(F^{n}B)\subset F^{n+p}C\kern 5.0pt\text{for all }n\,\}.

Similarly, give the
k
-module
k
the trivial filtration, and filter

B^{*}
by

F^{p}(B^{*}):=F^{p}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{k}(B,\,k):=\{\,\beta\colon B\to k\mid\beta(F^{n}B)\subset F^{n+p}k\kern 5.0pt\text{for all }n\,\}.\\

Then it’s easy to see that

F^{p}(B^{*})=\{\,\beta\colon B\to k\mid\beta(F^{n}B)=0\kern 5.0pt\text{for }n+p=1\,\}=(B/F^{1-p}B)^{*}.

(2.2.1)

Notice, however, that even if
F^{\bullet}B
is exhaustive,

F^{\bullet}(B^{*})
need not be exhaustive.

Given a map of filtered modules
\beta\colon B\to C
, its dual
\beta^{*}\colon C^{*}\to B^{*}
is, plainly, a map of filtered modules. Thus
C\mapsto C^{*}
is a contravariant, additive functor from
\mathbb{F}_{A}
to itself.

Filter the tensor product
B\otimes_{A}C
in
\mathbb{M}_{A}
by setting

\textstyle F^{p}(B\otimes_{A}C):=\mathop{\rm Im}(\tau)\text{\kern 5.0ptwhere }\tau\colon\bigoplus_{m+n=p}F^{m}(B)\otimes_{A}F^{n}(C)\to B\otimes_{A}C.

Then it’s easy to see that this
adjoint associativity formula

preserves filtrations:
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{k}(B\otimes_{A}C,\,k)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}\bigl(B,\,\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{k}(C,k)\bigr)
. In other words,

(B\otimes_{A}C)^{*}=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(B,\,C^{*})\text{\quad in }\mathbb{F}_{A}.

(2.2.2)

In particular, taking
C:=A
yields the following (duality) formula:

B^{*}=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(B,\,A^{*})\text{\quad in }\mathbb{F}_{A}.

(2.2.3)

Let now
A
be a graded
k
-algebra, and
C
a graded
A
-module. Say

A=\bigoplus_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}A_{p}
with
A_{p}=0
for
p<0
, and
C=\bigoplus_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}C_{p}
. For all
n
, set
F^{n}A:=\bigoplus_{p\geq n}A_{p}
and
F^{n}C:=\bigoplus_{p\geq n}C_{p}
. Plainly, the
F^{n}A
turn

A
into a filtered
k
-algebra, and the
F^{n}C
turn
C
into a filtered

A
-module; moreover,
G_{p}A=A_{p}
and
G_{p}C=C_{p}
for all
p
.

Also, an
A
-module map
\gamma\colon C\to D
is
homogeneous
(that

is,
D
is graded, say
D=\bigoplus D_{p}
, and
\gamma C_{p}\subset D_{p}

for all
p
) iff
\gamma\in\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\,\mathbb{F}_{A}}(B,\,C)
. Thus the category

\mathbb{G}_{A}
of graded modules and homogeneous maps is a full subcategory

of
\mathbb{F}_{A}
. And the functor
G_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{F}_{A}\to\mathbb{G}_{A}
, given by

C\mapsto G_{\bullet}C
, is a
retraction
; that is,
G_{\bullet}\big|\mathbb{G}_{A}=1
.

Let
B
be the kernel of
\gamma
in
\mathbb{M}_{A}
. Then
B
is naturally
graded:
B=\bigoplus_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}B_{p}
where
B_{P}:=B\cap C_{p}
. So
B

is the kernel of
\gamma
in
\mathbb{G}_{A}
. Moreover,
F^{n}B=B\cap F^{n}C

for all
n
; so
B
is the kernel of
\gamma
in
\mathbb{F}_{A}
by
(
2.3
). Similarly, the cokernel of
\gamma
in
\mathbb{M}_{A}
is
also its cokernel in
\mathbb{G}_{A}
and in
\mathbb{F}_{A}
. In particular,
\mathbb{G}_{A}
is, therefore, Abelian.

(2.3)  (Strict maps).

Fix a filtered
k
-algebra
(A,\,F^{\bullet}A)
. Following

[
SerreAlLoc
]
*p. II-2 (and
[
D71
]
*(1.1.5), p. 7, call a map

\gamma\colon(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)\to(D,\,F^{\bullet}D)
in
\mathbb{F}_{A}

strict

(or

strictly compatible with the filtrations
) if
\gamma(F^{n}C)=\gamma(C)\cap F^{n}D
for all
n
.

For example, if
\gamma\colon C\to D
is injective, then
\gamma
is strict
iff
F^{\bullet}C
is
induced
by
F^{\bullet}D
in the sense that
F^{n}C=\gamma^{-1}(F^{n}D)
. Instead, if
\gamma\colon C\to D
is surjective, then

\gamma
is strict iff
F^{\bullet}D
is
induced
by
F^{\bullet}C
in the
sense that
F^{n}D=\gamma(F^{n}C)
. In general,
\gamma
is strict iff both

F^{\bullet}C
and
F^{\bullet}D
induce the same filtration on
\gamma(C)
.

Let’s see
\gamma\colon(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)\to(D,\,F^{\bullet}D)

has a kernel

\mathop{\rm Ker}(\gamma)
and a cokernel

\mathop{\rm Cok}(\gamma)
in
\mathbb{F}_{A}
, whose
underlying
A
-modules are the usual
\mathop{\rm Ker}(\gamma)
and
\mathop{\rm Cok}(\gamma)

in
\mathbb{M}_{A}
, and whose filtrations are induced by
F^{\bullet}C
and
F^{\bullet}D
.
Given
\beta\colon(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)\to(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)
with
\gamma\beta=0
, there’s a unique
\alpha\colon B\to\mathop{\rm Ker}(\gamma)
in
\mathbb{M}_{A}
with

\mathop{\rm ker}(\gamma)\alpha=\beta
where
\mathop{\rm ker}(\gamma)\colon\mathop{\rm Ker}(\gamma)\hookrightarrow C
.
But
\mathop{\rm ker}(\gamma)(\alpha(F^{n}B))=\beta(F^{n}B)\subset F^{n}C
. Thus, as
desired,
\alpha(F^{n}B)\subset\mathop{\rm ker}(\gamma)^{-1}(F^{n}C)
. The analysis of

\mathop{\rm Cok}(\gamma)
is dual.

Consider the following sequence in
\mathbb{F}_{A}
associated to
\gamma\colon(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)\to(D,\,F^{\bullet}D)
:

\mathop{\rm Ker}(\gamma)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm ker}(\gamma)}C\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm coim}(\gamma)}\mathop{\rm Coim}(\gamma)\xrightarrow{\theta(\gamma)}\mathop{\rm Im}(\gamma)\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm im}(\gamma)}D\xrightarrow{\mathop{\rm cok}(\gamma)}\mathop{\rm Cok}(\gamma)

where
\mathop{\rm coim}(\gamma):=\mathop{\rm cok}(\mathop{\rm ker}(\gamma))
and
\mathop{\rm im}(\gamma):=\mathop{\rm ker}(\mathop{\rm cok}(\gamma))
. Note
\theta(\gamma)
is bijective in
\mathbb{M}_{A}
,
and it’s an isomorphism in
\mathbb{F}_{A}
iff
F^{\bullet}C
and
F^{\bullet}D
induce
the same filtration on
\mathop{\rm Im}(\gamma)
, so iff
\gamma
is strict. Thus,
since not every
\gamma
is strict,
\mathbb{F}_{A}
fails to be Abelian.

Given
(E,\,F^{n}E)
in
\mathbb{F}_{A}
, let’s see
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(\bullet,\,E)
is left
exact in the sense that

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(\,\mathop{\rm Cok}(\gamma),\,E\,)=\mathop{\rm Ker}(\,\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(\gamma,\,E)\,)\text{\quad in\kern 5.0pt}\mathbb{F}_{A}.

(2.3.1)

First, (
2.3.1
) holds in
\mathbb{M}_{A}
, as each side can be
canonically identified with the set of
A
-maps
\delta\colon D\to E
with

\delta\gamma=0
. Second, (
2.3.1
) holds in
\mathbb{F}_{A}
, as the

p
th term of the filtration of each side is identified with the subset
of all
\delta\colon D\to E[p]
in
\mathbb{F}_{A}
.

Finally, it is clear from the construction of
\mathop{\rm Cok}(\gamma)
that it’s
not only the cokernel in
\mathbb{F}_{A}
, but also in
\mathbb{F}_{k}
. Hence

\mathop{\rm Cok}(\gamma)^{*}=\mathop{\rm Ker}(\gamma^{*})
in
\mathbb{F}_{k}
by (
2.3.1
) with
A:=k
and
E:=k
. In particular, given
n
, then
F^{n}(\mathop{\rm Cok}(\gamma)^{*})=F^{n}(\mathop{\rm Ker}(\gamma^{*}))
in
\mathbb{M}_{k}
; so, this equation holds in
\mathbb{M}_{A}
too, as it plainly respects multiplication by each
a\in A
. Thus

\mathop{\rm Cok}(\gamma)^{*}=\mathop{\rm Ker}(\gamma^{*})\text{\quad in\kern 5.0pt}\mathbb{F}_{A}.

(2.3.2)

(2.4)  (Rees modules).

Fix a filtered
k
-algebra
(A,\,F^{\bullet}A)
and a variable
t
. Form
the
(extended) Rees algebra

\mathop{\rm Rees}(A):=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}F^{n}A\cdot t^{-n}
and the
Rees functor

\textstyle\mathop{\rm Rees}\colon\mathbb{F}_{A}\to\mathbb{G}_{\mathop{\rm Rees}(A)}\text{\quad by\quad}(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)\mapsto\mathop{\rm Rees}(B):=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}F^{n}B\cdot t^{-n}.

Note that
\mathbb{F}_{A}
and
\mathbb{EF}_{A}
have the same image
\mathbb{R}_{A}
,
which (see
[
LO96
]
*p. vi) is the full subcategory of graded

\mathop{\rm Rees}(A)
-modules on which
t
is
regular
(that is,
multiplication by
t
is injective); an
M:=\bigoplus M_{n}
in
\mathbb{R}_{A}
is equal to the image of
(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)
where
B:=M/(1-t)M
and

F^{n}B:=(M_{n}+(1-t)M)/(1-t)M
is the image of
M_{n}
in
B
. Moreover,
the Rees functor embeds
\mathbb{EF}
as
\mathbb{R}_{A}
in the Abelian category

\mathbb{G}_{\mathop{\rm Rees}(A)}
.

Notice that
\mathbb{R}_{A}
is closed under extensions; that is, given a short
exact sequence
0\to M\to M^{\prime}\to M^{\prime\prime}\to 0
in
\mathbb{G}_{\mathop{\rm Rees}(A)}
, if

M,\ M^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{A}
, then
M^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{A}
; indeed, apply the Snake
Lemma to this diagram, where the
\tau
’s denote multiplication by
t
,

\kern 2.5pt\kern-2.5pt0\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}M\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}M^{\prime}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}M^{\prime\prime}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}0

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\tau$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\tau^{\prime}$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\tau^{\prime\prime}$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\kern 2.5pt\kern-2.5pt0\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}M\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}M^{\prime}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}M^{\prime\prime}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}0

(2.4.1)

in order to see that
t
is regular on
M^{\prime}
because it is on
M
and

M^{\prime\prime}
.

Thus
\mathbb{R}_{A}
is the seminal example of an
exact category
in

[
Q
]
*p. 99. Accordingly, call a sequence
0\to(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)\xrightarrow{\beta}(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)\xrightarrow{\gamma}(D,\,F^{\bullet}D)\to 0
in
\mathbb{EF}_{A}

exact
if its image in
\mathbb{G}_{\mathop{\rm Rees}(A)}
is exact. Plainly, the
latter condition holds iff each sequence
0\to F^{n}B\to F^{n}C\to F^{n}D\to 0
is exact in
\mathbb{M}_{A}
; so iff
F^{\bullet}B
and
F^{\bullet}D
are induced by

F^{\bullet}C
; so iff
\beta=\mathop{\rm ker}(\gamma)
and
\gamma=\mathop{\rm cok}(\beta)
in

\mathbb{EF}_{A}
, or equivalently in
\mathbb{F}_{A}
.

Given
(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)
in
\mathbb{F}_{A}
, notice that

G_{\bullet}B=\mathop{\rm Rees}(B)\bigm/t\cdot\mathop{\rm Rees}(B)=\mathop{\rm Rees}(B)\otimes_{\mathop{\rm Rees}(A)}G_{\bullet}A.

(2.4.2)

Thus the functor
G_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{F}_{A}\to\mathbb{G}_{G_{\bullet}A}
factors through

\bullet\otimes G_{\bullet}A\colon\mathbb{G}_{\mathop{\rm Rees}(A)}\to\mathbb{G}_{G_{\bullet}A}
.

The latter functor is, of course, right exact. Moreover, its
restriction to
\mathbb{R}_{A}
is exact owing to the 9-Lemma applied to
(
2.4.1
). Thus
G_{\bullet}
is exact on
\mathbb{EF}_{A}
in the sense that
it preserves short exact sequences (compare with
[
Bour
]
*Prp. 2,
p. 25).

Let’s say that the restriction of
G_{\bullet}
to a subcategory
\mathbb{S}
of

\mathbb{EF}_{A}
is
biexact
if a sequence
0\to(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)\xrightarrow{\beta}(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)\xrightarrow{\gamma}(D,\,F^{\bullet}D)\to 0
in
\mathbb{S}
with

\gamma\beta=0
is exact in
\mathbb{EF}_{A}
when its image under
G_{\bullet}

is exact in
\mathbb{G}_{G_{\bullet}A}
.

By (
2.5
) below, the restriction of
G_{\bullet}
to
\mathbb{S}
is
biexact if
\mathbb{S}
is
fully separated
in the sense that
\mathbb{S}

is closed under taking kernels and cokernels, and if
(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)\in\mathbb{S}
, then
F^{\bullet}B
is separated. For example,
\mathbb{DEF}_{A}
is,
plainly, fully separated.

For another example, take
A
to be a
Zariski ring
(see

[
ZSvII
]
*§ 4, p. 261 and
[
LO96
]
*p. 83); namely,

\mathop{\rm Rees}(A)
is a Noetherian ring, and
F^{1}A
lies in the Jacobson radical

\mathop{\rm Jac}(A
). For instance,
A
could be a weighted power series ring over

k
. Let
\mathbb{ZF}_{A}
be the full subcategory of
\mathbb{EF}_{A}
of

(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)
such that
\mathop{\rm Rees}(B)
is a finitely generated

\mathop{\rm Rees}(A)
-module.
Plainly,
\mathbb{ZF}_{A}
is closed under taking kernels and cokernels.

Given
(B,\,F^{\bullet}B)
in
\mathbb{Z}F_{A}
and
b\in\bigcap_{n}F^{n}B
, let’s see

b=0
. Inspired by Rees (see
[
Sharp
]
*p. 563), set
M^{(n)}:=\mathop{\rm Rees}(A)\cdot bt^{-n}\subset\mathop{\rm Rees}(B)
. The
M^{(n)}
form an
increasing chain. As
\mathop{\rm Rees}(B)
is Noetherian,
M^{(p)}=M^{(p+1)}
for
some
p
. So
ubt^{-p}=bt^{-(p+1)}
for some
u\in\mathop{\rm Rees}(A)
. Say
u=\sum u_{i}t^{-i}
with
u_{i}\in F^{i}A
. Then
u_{1}b=b
. So
(1-u_{1})b=0
. But
F^{1}A\subset\mathop{\rm Jac}(A)
. So
b=0
. Thus
\mathbb{ZF}_{A}
is fully
separated.

(2.5) Proposition.

Let
A
be a filtered
k
-algebra, and
\mathbb{S}\subset\mathbb{EF}_{A}
a fully
separated subcategory. Then
G_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{EF}_{A}\to\mathbb{G}_{G_{\bullet}A}
is
exact, and its restriction to
\mathbb{S}
is biexact.

Proof.

It remains to see that a sequence
0\to M\xrightarrow{\mu}M^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mu^{\prime}}M^{\prime\prime}\to 0

in the image of
\mathbb{S}
in
\mathbb{R}_{A}
is exact if
\mu^{\prime}\mu=0
and if

0\to M/tM\xrightarrow{\bar{\mu}}M^{\prime}/tM^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\bar{\mu}^{\prime}}M^{\prime\prime}/tM^{\prime\prime}\to 0
is exact in

\mathbb{G}_{G_{\bullet}A}
. Consider the following commutative diagram with
exact rows:

\kern 2.5pt\kern-2.5pt0\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to17.2359pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}M\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to25.03142pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits^{\tau}$}M\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to19.20815pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}M/tM\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to17.80537pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}0

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mu$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mu$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\bar{\mu}$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\kern 2.5pt\kern-2.5pt0\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}M^{\prime}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to22.22588pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits^{\tau^{\prime}}$}M^{\prime}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}M^{\prime}/tM^{\prime}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}0

(2.5.1)

where the
\tau
’s denote multiplication by
t
.

Set
L:=\mathop{\rm Ker}(\mu)
. Since
\bar{\mu}
is injective, multiplication by

t
on
L
is bijective by the Snake Lemma; so if say
L=\bigoplus L_{n}
, then
L_{n+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}L_{n}
for all
n
. But
L
is in the image of

\mathbb{S}
, and
\mathbb{S}
is fully separated. Thus
L=0
.

Set
N:=\mathop{\rm Cok}(\mu)
. As
\mu^{\prime}\mu=0
, there’s a commutative diagram
with exact rows

\kern 2.5pt\kern-2.5pt0\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to19.22203pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}N\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to31.20856pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits^{\theta}$}N\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to26.4582pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}N/tN\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to23.0693pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}0

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\nu$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\nu$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\bar{\nu}$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\kern 2.5pt\kern-2.5pt0\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}M^{\prime\prime}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to24.43076pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits^{\tau^{\prime\prime}}$}M^{\prime\prime}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}M^{\prime\prime}/tM^{\prime\prime\prime}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}0

(2.5.2)

where
\theta
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}
denote multiplication by
t
. Note that,
applied to (
2.5.1
), the Snake Lemma yields
\mathop{\rm Cok}(\bar{\mu})=N/tN
.

By Hypothesis,
\mathop{\rm Cok}(\bar{\mu})=M^{\prime\prime}/tM^{\prime\prime}
. Hence
\bar{\nu}
is bijective.
So applied to (
2.5.2
), the Snake Lemma yields that
multiplication by
t
is bijective on
\mathop{\rm Ker}(\nu)
and on
\mathop{\rm Cok}(\nu)
.
So arguing as for
L
yields
\mathop{\rm Ker}(\nu)=0
and
\mathop{\rm Cok}(\nu)=0
. Thus

\nu
is a isomorphism. Thus
0\to M\to M^{\prime}\to M^{\prime\prime}\to 0
is exact, as
desired.
∎

(2.6)  (
k
-Artinian modules).

Fix a
k
-algebra
A
.

Call an
A
-module
C

k
-Artinian
if
C
is locally free of

constant finite rank over
k
. Let
\mathbb{AM}_{A}\subset\mathbb{M}_{A}
denote

the full subcategory of such
C
. Note that
\mathbb{AM}_{A}
is
A
-linear,
but not Abelian. Given
C\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
, note, plainly,
C^{*}\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
too. Thus
*\colon\mathbb{M}_{A}\to\mathbb{M}_{A}
restricts to an
A
-linear
functor
*\colon\mathbb{AM}_{A}\to\mathbb{AM}_{A}
.

When
k
is a field, every
C\in\mathbb{M}_{A}
is free. So
C
is

k
-Artinian iff
\dim_{k}C<\infty
.

Given a function
\mathbf{h}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
, define
\mathbf{h}^{*}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
by
\mathbf{h}^{*}(n):=\mathbf{h}(-n)
. Also, set

i(\mathbf{h}):=\inf\{\,n\mid\mathbf{h}(n)\neq 0\,\}s(\mathbf{h}):=\sup\{\,n\mid\mathbf{h}(n)\neq 0\,\}.

Call
\mathbf{h}

finite
if
i(\mathbf{h})
and
s(\mathbf{h})
are finite (so
if
h\neq 0
, then
\{\,n\mid\mathbf{h}(n)\neq 0\,\}
is finite).

Assume
A
is graded. Given
C\in\mathbb{G}_{A}
, say
C=\bigoplus C_{n}
.
Notice that, if
C
is
k
-Artinian, then
C_{n}=0
for almost all
n
,
and each
C_{n}
is locally free of finite, but not necessarily constant,
rank; the converse holds if also the rank of each
C_{n}
is constant.
Call
\mathbf{h}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
the
Hilbert function
of
C
if each

C_{n}
is locally free of (constant) rank
\mathbf{h}(n)
; let
\mathbb{AG}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}}
denote the collection of all such
C
.

Let
\mathbb{AG}_{A}\subset\mathbb{G}_{A}
denote the full subcategory of all
C\in\mathbb{AG}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}}
for all finite
\mathbf{h}
.

Assume
A
is just filtered. Given
(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)\in\mathbb{F}_{A}
, call
C

k
-Artinian with Hilbert function
\mathbf{h}
if
G_{\bullet}C\in\mathbb{AG}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}}
and if
F^{\bullet}C
is finite; let
\mathbb{AF}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}}

denote the collection of all such
(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)
. Now,
F^{n}C=F^{n+1}C
for
n<i(\mathbf{h})
and
n>s(\mathbf{h})
; so
F^{\bullet}C
is finite
iff
F^{n}C=C
for
n\leq i(\mathbf{h})
and
F^{n}C=0
for
n>s(\mathbf{h})
.

Let
\mathbb{AF}_{A}\subset\mathbb{F}_{A}
denote the full subcategory of all

(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)
with
G_{\bullet}C\in\mathbb{AG}_{A}
. Trivially, the functor

G_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{F}_{A}\to\mathbb{G}_{G_{\bullet}A}
restricts to a functor
G_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{AF}_{A}\to\mathbb{AG}_{G_{\bullet}A}
. Trivially,
\mathbb{AF}_{A}
is
exhaustive and discrete. Thus, (
2.5
) implies
G_{\bullet}
is
biexact.

As a consequence, a
(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
is generated over
A

by a
k
-submodule
N
with the induced filtration if
G_{\bullet}N

generates
G_{\bullet}C
over
G_{\bullet}A
. Indeed, first note
G_{\bullet}N\subset G_{\bullet}C
as
G_{\bullet}
is exact. Now, form the multiplication map
\mu\colon A\otimes_{k}N\to C
. The induced map
G_{\bullet}\mu\colon G_{\bullet}A\otimes_{k}G_{\bullet}N\to G_{\bullet}C
is surjective by hypothesis. Thus, as
G_{\bullet}
is biexact,

\mu
is surjective, as desired.

Finally, call
A

k
-Gorenstein
if
A^{*}
is a locally free of

rank 1 (that is, invertible).

(2.7) Proposition.

Let
A
be a filtered
k
-algebra, and
(C,\,F^{\bullet}C)\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}}
.

(1)
Then
C
and
C/F^{n}C
and
F^{m}C
and
F^{m}C/F^{n}C
are, for all
m\leq n
, locally free of ranks
\sum_{p}\mathbf{h}(p)
and
\sum_{p<n}\mathbf{h}(p)
and

\sum_{m\leq p}\mathbf{h}(p)
and
\sum_{m\leq p<n}\mathbf{h}(p)
.

(2)
Then
F^{n}((C^{*})^{*})=F^{n}C
for all
n
.

(3)
Then
G_{n}(C^{*})=(G_{-n}C)^{*}
for all
n
.

(4)
Then
C^{*}\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
.

Proof.

For (1),
fix
m
. If
m=n
, then
F^{m}C/F^{n}C=0
. Suppose
m<n
.
Form the exact sequence
0\to G_{n}C\to F^{m}C/F^{n+1}C\to F^{m}C/F^{n}C\to 0
.
By hypothesis,
G_{n}C
is locally free of rank
\mathbf{h}(n)
. By induction,
assume
F^{m}C/F^{n}C
is locally free of rank
\sum_{m\leq p<n}\mathbf{h}(p)
.
Thus
F^{m}C/F^{n+1}C
is locally free of rank
\sum_{m\leq p<n+1}\mathbf{h}(p)
. But
F^{m}C=C
for
m\leq i(\mathbf{h})
and
F^{n}C=0
for
n>s(\mathbf{h})
. Thus (1) holds.

For (2),
note
F^{n}((C^{*})^{*}):=(C^{*}/F^{1-n}(C^{*}))^{*}
and

F^{1-n}(C^{*}):=(C/F^{n}C)^{*}
. So form the exact sequence
0\to F^{n}C\to C\to C/F^{n}C\to 0
of
k
-modules. It splits, as
C/F^{n}C
is projective
by (1). So
0\to(C/F^{n}C)^{*}\to C^{*}\to(F^{n}C)^{*}\to 0
is exact. Hence

(F^{n}C)^{*}=C^{*}/F^{1-n}(C^{*})
. Thus
F^{n}((C^{*})^{*})=((F^{n}C)^{*})^{*}
. But

((F^{n}C)^{*})^{*}=F^{n}C
as
F^{n}C
is locally free of finite rank by (1).
Thus (2) holds.

For (3),
form the exact sequence
0\to G_{n}C\to C/F^{n+1}C\to C/F^{n}C\to 0
. Note that
C/F^{n}C
is projective by (1). So replace
n

by
-n
, and dualize. Thus
0\to(C/F^{-n}C)^{*}\to(C/F^{1-n}C)^{*}\to(G_{-n}C)^{*}\to 0
is exact. But the first term is equal to

F^{n+1}(C^{*})
, and the second, to
F^{n}(C^{*})
. Thus (3) holds.

For (4),
note (3) yields
G_{\bullet}(C^{*})\in\mathbb{AG}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
. Also,

F^{n}(C^{*}):=(C/F^{1-n}C)^{*}
for all
n
by (
2.2.1
); so
F^{\bullet}(C^{*})
is finite as
F^{\bullet}C
is.
∎

(2.8) Proposition.

Let
A
be a filtered
k
-algebra, and
0\to B\xrightarrow{\beta}C\xrightarrow{\gamma}D\to 0
an exact sequence in
\mathbb{EF}_{A}
.

(1)
Assume
D\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
. Then
B\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
iff
C\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
.

(2)
Assume
B,\,C,\,D\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
. Then
0\to D^{*}\xrightarrow{\gamma^{*}}C^{*}\xrightarrow{\beta^{*}}B^{*}\to 0
is exact in
\mathbb{EF}_{A}
.

Proof.

For (1),
given
n
, note
G_{n}(D)
is locally free of constant finite
rank. Hence,
G_{n}(B)
is so iff
G_{n}(C)
is so, since
0\to G_{n}(B)\to G_{n}(C)\to G_{n}(D)\to 0
is exact by (
2.5
). Also,
F^{\bullet}B
is
finite iff
F^{\bullet}C
is, since
F^{\bullet}D
is. Thus (1) holds.

For (2),
given
n
, set
p:=1-n
, and form the following commutative
diagram:

\kern 2.5pt\kern-2.5pt0\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to25.94293pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}D^{*}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to36.05273pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}C^{*}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to36.05273pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}B^{*}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to25.94293pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}0

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\kern 2.5pt\kern-2.5pt0\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}(F^{p}D)^{*}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}(F^{p}C)^{*}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}(F^{p}B)^{*}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to15.83313pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits$}0

(2.8.1)

The upper sequence is exact, since
0\to B\to C\to D\to 0
splits as

D
is projective by (
2.7
)(1). Similarly, the lower sequence
is exact.

The sequence
0\to F^{p}D\to D\to D/F^{p}D\to 0
splits as
D/F^{p}D
is
projective by (
2.7
)(1). So the left map in (
2.8.1
) is
surjective, and its kernel is
(D/F^{p}D)^{*}
, or
F^{n}(D^{*})
by
(
2.2.1
). Similarly, the middle and right maps are surjective, and
their kernels are
F^{n}(C^{*})
and
F^{n}(B^{*})
. Hence
0\to F^{n}(D^{*})\to F^{n}(C^{*})\to F^{n}(B^{*})\to 0
is exact by the 9-Lemma. Thus (2) holds.
∎

3.
Dualities

(3.1) Setup.

Keep the setup of (
2.1
). For a
k
-algebra
K
and a
k
-module

C
, set

C_{K}:=C\otimes_{k}K.

Let
A
be a filtered
k
-algebra, and
C\in\mathbb{F}_{A}
. Given a

k
-algebra
K
, note that
C_{K}
carries an induced filtration; by
definition,
F^{n}(C_{K})
is the image of
(F^{n}C)_{K}
in
C_{K}
.

Given a contravariant functor
\mathbf{D}
from an additive category
\mathbb{S}

to itself, call
\mathbf{D}
