dualizing
if
\mathbf{D}^{2}
is naturally

isomorphic to the identity functor. Trivially,
\mathbf{D}
is an
equivalence of
\mathbb{S}
with its opposite category.

Call an object
D
of
\mathbb{S}

dualizing
if
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{S}}(C,\,D)
is in
\mathbb{S}
for any
C\in\mathbb{S}
and if the functor
C\mapsto\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{S}}(C,\,D)
is dualizing. More generally, given a forgetful
functor from
\mathbb{S}
to another additive category
\mathbb{T}
, call an
object
D
of
\mathbb{S}

dualizing for

(\mathbb{S},\,\mathbb{T})
if

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{T}}(C,\,D)
is in
\mathbb{S}
for any
C\in\mathbb{S}
and if

C\mapsto\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{T}}(C,\,D)
is dualizing for
\mathbb{S}
.

(3.2) Proposition.

Let
A
be a
k
-algebra. Then
*\colon\mathbb{AM}_{A}\to\mathbb{AM}_{A}
is a
dualizing functor. If
A
is filtered, then
*
induces two more
dualizing functors:
*\colon\mathbb{AF}_{A}\to\mathbb{AF}_{A}
and
*\colon\mathbb{AG}_{G_{\bullet}A}\to\mathbb{AG}_{G_{\bullet}A}
; the latter two
*
’s commute
with
G_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{AF}_{A}\to\mathbb{AG}_{G_{\bullet}A}
.

Moreover, if
A
is filtered, then for any
B,\,C\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
,

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(B,\,C)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C^{*},\,B^{*})\text{\hskip 10.22217ptin\kern 5.11108pt}\mathbb{F}_{A}.

(3.2.1)

Proof.

Given
C\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
, note that the natural map
C\to(C^{*})^{*}
is an
isomorphism. Thus
*\colon\mathbb{AM}_{A}\to\mathbb{AM}_{A}
is a dualizing functor.

Assume
A
is filtered. Given
C\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
, note
C\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
by
(
2.7
)(1); moreover,
C^{*}\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
by (
2.7
)(4).
Thus the functor
*\colon\mathbb{AM}_{A}\to\mathbb{AM}_{A}
induces a functor

*\colon\mathbb{AF}_{A}\to\mathbb{AF}_{A}
, which is dualizing by (
2.7
)(2).

Given
B,\,C\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
, note that, as
*\colon\mathbb{AF}_{A}\to\mathbb{AF}_{A}
is
dualizing,

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\,\mathbb{F}_{A}}(B,\,C)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\,\mathbb{F}_{A}}(C^{*},\,B^{*})\text{\quad in\kern 5.0pt}\mathbb{F}_{A}.

(3.2.2)

Given
p
, note
C^{*}[p]=(C[-p])^{*}
. Hence, replacing
C
by
C[p]

in (
3.2.2
) yields

F^{p}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(B,\,C)=F^{p}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C^{*},\,B^{*}).

But
F^{\bullet}B
and
F^{\bullet}C
are finite; so
F^{\bullet}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(B,\,C)
and
F^{\bullet}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C^{*},\,B^{*})
are finite too. Thus (
3.2.1
) holds.

Given
C\in\mathbb{AG}_{G_{\bullet}A}
, say
C=\bigoplus_{p}C_{p}
. Note
C^{*}=\bigoplus_{q}(C_{-q})^{*}
; so
C^{*}\in\mathbb{AG}_{G_{\bullet}A}
. Thus

*\colon\mathbb{AM}_{A}\to\mathbb{AM}_{A}
restricts to a dualizing functor

*\colon\mathbb{AG}_{A}\to\mathbb{AG}_{A}
. These two functors commute with
G_{\bullet}

owing to (
2.7
)(3).
∎

(3.3) Proposition
(Relative Matlis Duality)
.

Let
A
be a
k
-Artinian
k
-algebra. Then
A^{*}
is dualizing and
injective for
\mathbb{AM}_{A}
.

Assume
A\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
. Then
A^{*}
is dualizing for

(\mathbb{AF}_{A},\,\mathbb{M}_{A})
, and
(G_{\bullet}A)^{*}
is dualizing for

(\mathbb{AG}_{G_{\bullet}A},\,\mathbb{M}_{G_{\bullet}A})
. Moreover,
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{G_{\bullet}A}\bigl(G_{\bullet}C,\,(G_{\bullet}A)^{*}\bigr)=G_{\bullet}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A^{*})
for any

C
in
\mathbb{AF}_{A}
, and
(G_{\bullet}A)^{*}=G_{\bullet}(A^{*})
. Lastly, if also

G_{0}A=k
, then
k
embeds in
A^{*}
as the smallest nonzero submodule
D

with
A^{*}/D\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
.

Proof.

Note
A^{*}\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
and
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbb{AM}_{A}}(C,\,A^{*})=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A^{*})
for
C\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
; see (
2.6
). However,

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A^{*})=C^{*}
by (
2.2.3
) with
A
and
C
filtered
trivially, and
*
is dualizing for
\mathbb{AM}_{A}
by (
3.2
).
Thus
A^{*}
is a dualizing module for
\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. In
\mathbb{AM}_{A}
,
moreover,
A^{*}
is injective, as
C\mapsto C^{*}
converts projectives
into injectives.

Assume
A\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
. Then, similarly, (
3.2
) and
(
2.2.3
) imply that
A^{*}
is dualizing for
(\mathbb{AF}_{A},\,\mathbb{M}_{A})
, and
(G_{\bullet}A)^{*}
is dualizing for
(\mathbb{AG}_{G_{\bullet}A},\,\mathbb{M}_{G_{\bullet}A})
.

Moreover,
(G_{\bullet}C)^{*}=G_{\bullet}(C^{*})
by (
2.7
)(3). Apply
(
2.2.3
) to
G_{\bullet}A
and to
A
. Thus
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{G_{\bullet}A}(G_{\bullet}C,\,(G_{\bullet}A)^{*})=G_{\bullet}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A^{*})
. On the other hand, taking
C:=A
yields
(G_{\bullet}A)^{*}=G_{\bullet}(A^{*})
.

Lastly, let
\delta\colon D\hookrightarrow A^{*}
be the inclusion of a nonzero
submodule with
A^{*}/D
in
\mathbb{AF}_{A}
. Then
D\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
by
(
2.8
)(1). Set
C:=D^{*}
. Then
C\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
by
(
2.7
)(4). So
G_{0}C
is locally free. Moreover,
\delta^{*}\colon A\to C
is surjective and
C
has the induced filtration by
(
2.8
)(2) and (
2.3
). So
G_{0}A\to G_{0}C
is well defined
and surjective. But
G_{0}C\neq 0
, as
1\in G_{0}C
and as
1\neq 0
since

G_{\bullet}C
had the same rank as
C
.

Assume
G_{0}A=k
. Then, by the above,
G_{0}A\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}G_{0}C
. Hence the
quotient map
\kappa\colon A\twoheadrightarrow k
factors via
\delta^{*}\colon A\twoheadrightarrow C
. So

\kappa^{*}:k\hookrightarrow A^{*}
factors via
\delta\colon D\hookrightarrow A^{*}
. Now,
F^{1}A\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
; so
A^{*}/k=(F^{1}A)^{*}
by (
2.8
)(2), and
(F^{1}A)^{*}\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
by (
2.7
)(4). Thus
\kappa^{*}
embeds
k
in
A^{*}
as
the smallest nonzero submodule
D
with
A^{*}/D\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
.
∎

(3.4)  (Apolarity).

Assume
A
is a graded
k
-algebra. Let
\mathbf{b}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
a function,
and
B
a graded
A
-module whose graded pieces
B_{p}
are locally free
of rank
\mathbf{b}(p)
for all
p
. Set
(B^{\dagger})_{q}:=(B_{-q})^{*}
and

B^{\dagger}:=\bigoplus_{q}(B^{\dagger})_{q}
. Note that
B^{*}=\prod(B^{\dagger})_{q}
,
that
B^{\dagger}
is an
A
-submodule of
B^{*}
, that
B^{\dagger}
is graded, and
that
(B^{\dagger})_{q}
is locally free of rank
\mathbf{b}^{*}(q)
for all
q
.
Often,
B^{\dagger}
is called the
graded dual
of
B
.

Given any
A
-submodule
I
of
B
, its
apolar annihilator

(0:_{B^{\dagger}}I)
is defined by

(0:_{B^{\dagger}}I):=\{\,f\in B^{\dagger}\mid\psi\cdot f=0\text{ for all }\psi\in I\,\}.

Note that
(0:_{B^{\dagger}}I)
is an
A
-submodule of
B^{\dagger}
.

Note
B^{\dagger\dagger}=B
. Hence, given any
A
-submodule
D
of

B^{\dagger}
,

(0:_{B}D)=\{\,\psi\in B\mid\psi\cdot f=0\text{ for all }f\in D\,\}.

Equip
B
and
B^{\dagger}
with the filtrations arising from their
gradings; equip every subquotient of
B
and
B^{\dagger}
with the
induced filtration. Fix a finite function
\mathbf{h}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
.

Denote by
\Psi_{B}
the set of
A
-submodules
I\subset B
with
B/I\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. Denote by
\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}
the set of
A
-submodules

D\subset B^{\dagger}
with
D\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
and with
B^{\dagger}/D
flat over

k
.

Denote by
\mathbf{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\subset\Psi_{B}
the subset of
I

with
B/I\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}}
, and by
\mathbf{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}\subset\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}
the subset of
D
with
D\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
and with
G_{\bullet}(E)
flat over
k
where
E:=B^{\dagger}/D
. Note that
E
is flat over
k
when
G_{\bullet}(E)
is, as
E=\bigl(\bigoplus_{p\notin[i,\,s]}B^{\dagger}_{-p}\bigr)\oplus(F^{-s}E/F^{-i+1}E)
with
i:=i(\mathbf{h})
and
s:=s(\mathbf{h})
and as

F^{-s}E/F^{-i+1}E
is flat by the proof of (
2.7
)(1).

Denote by
\mathbf{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\subset\mathbf{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
and

\mathbf{H}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}\subset\mathbf{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
the subset of homogeneous
I
and the
subset of homogeneous
D
.

(3.5) Theorem
(Macaulay Duality)
.

Keep the setup of (
3.4
).

(1)
Then
I\mapsto(0:_{B^{\dagger}}I)
gives a bijection
\Psi_{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}
, and
D\mapsto(0:_{B}D)
, its inverse. Also,

(0:_{B^{\dagger}}I)=(B/I)^{*}
and
B/(0:_{B}D)=D^{*}
.

Further, if
B=A
and if
I
and
D
correspond, then
A/I
is a

k
-algebra, and
D
is a dualizing module for
\mathbb{AM}_{A/I}
; also,
then
A/I
is
k
-Gorenstein iff, for every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of

k
, there’s a field
K
containing
k/\mathfrak{m}
such that
D_{K}
is

A_{K}
-cyclic.

(2)
The bijection in
(1)
induces another,
\mathbf{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\mathbf{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
. Further, if
A=B
and
if
I
and
D
correspond, then
D
is a dualizing module for

(\mathbb{AF}_{A/I},\,\mathbb{AM}_{A/I})
.

The second bijection restricts to a third,
\mathbf{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\mathbf{H}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
. These two bijections
commute with taking associated graded modules. Furthermore, if
A=B

and if
I
and
D
correspond, then
D
is a dualizing module for

(\mathbb{AG}_{A/I},\,\mathbb{AM}_{A/I})
.

Proof.

For (1),
given
I\in\Psi_{B}
, set
C:=B/I
. Note
0\to I\to B\to C\to 0
splits over
k
as
C\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. So
0\to C^{*}\to B^{*}\to I^{*}\to 0
splits over
k
. Correspondingly, there’s a retraction

\rho\colon B^{*}\twoheadrightarrow C^{*}
.

As
C\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
, there are
m
and
n
with
G_{p}C=0
for
p<m

and
p>n
. Hence
B_{p}
maps to
0
in
C
for
p<m
and
p>n
. So

\bigoplus_{p=m}^{n}B_{p}
surjects onto
C
. Therefore,

\textstyle C^{*}\subset\bigoplus_{p=m}^{n}(B_{p})^{*}\subset B^{\dagger}\subset B^{*}.

Note
\rho|C^{*}=1
. So
\rho|B^{\dagger}
splits
0\to C^{*}\to B^{\dagger}\to B^{\dagger}/C^{*}\to 0
over
k
. Thus
B^{\dagger}/C^{*}
is flat over

k
, as
B^{\dagger}
is. But
B^{\dagger}/C^{*}\subset B^{*}/C^{*}=I^{*}
. So

f\in B^{\dagger}
maps to
0
in
I^{*}
iff
f\in C^{*}
; thus
C^{*}=(0:_{B^{\dagger}}I)
.

However,
C\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. So plainly
C^{*}\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. Thus

C^{*}\in\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}
.

Conversely, given
D\in\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}
, note
D
is finitely
generated over
k
as
D\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. So there are
m,\,n
with

D\subset E:=\bigoplus_{p=m}^{n}(B^{\dagger})_{p}
. Set
E^{\prime}:=\bigoplus_{p\notin[m,\,n]}(B^{\dagger})_{p}
, so
B^{\dagger}=E\oplus E^{\prime}
.
Then
B^{\dagger}/D=(E/D)\oplus E^{\prime}
. But
B^{\dagger}/D
is flat. So

E/D
is flat, so projective. Thus the inclusion
D\hookrightarrow E
induces a
surjection
E^{*}\twoheadrightarrow D^{*}
.

Plainly the surjection
(B^{\dagger})^{*}\twoheadrightarrow E^{*}
restricts to a
surjection
(B^{\dagger})^{\dagger}\twoheadrightarrow E^{*}
. But
(B^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=B
. Thus the the inclusion
D\hookrightarrow B^{\dagger}
induces a surjection

B\twoheadrightarrow D^{*}
; also, it’s not just a
k
-map, but an
A
-map.

Set
I:=\mathop{\rm Ker}(B\twoheadrightarrow D^{*})
. Then
I
is an
A
-submodule of
B
, as

B\twoheadrightarrow D^{*}
is an
A
-map. Further,
B/I=D^{*}
. But
D\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
, so
D^{*}\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. Thus
I\in\Psi_{B}
. Also,
\psi\in B
maps to
0
in
D^{*}
iff
\psi\in I
; so
\psi\cdot f=0
for
all
f\in D
. Thus
I=(0:_{B}D)
.

Next, let’s check that the functions
I\mapsto(B/I)^{*}
and
D\mapsto\mathop{\rm Ker}(B\twoheadrightarrow D^{*})
are inverse. Given
I
, set
D:=(B/I)^{*}
. Then
D^{*}=B/I
as
B/I\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. Thus
\mathop{\rm Ker}(B\twoheadrightarrow D^{*})=I
.
Conversely, given
D
, set
I:=\mathop{\rm Ker}(B\twoheadrightarrow D^{*})
. Then
B/I=D^{*}
.
So
(B/I)^{*}=D^{**}
. But
D^{**}=D
as
D\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. Thus

(B/I)^{*}=D
, as desired.

Further, assume
A=B
and
I
and
D
correspond. Set
C:=A/I
.
Then
C
is, plainly, a
k
-algebra, and
D
is a dualizing module for

\mathbb{AM}_{C}
by (
3.3
), as
D=C^{*}
.

Also, by (
5.2
), the
k
-algebra
C
is
k
-Gorenstein iff, for
every
K
, the
C_{K}
-module
(C^{*})_{K}
is cyclic, or equivalently,

(C^{*})_{K}
is
A_{K}
-cyclic. But
D=C^{*}
. Thus (1) holds,

For (2),
given
I\in\mathbf{F}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
, set
C:=B/I\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Note
C^{*}\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
by
(
2.7
)(4).

Fix
p
. As
G_{p}C
is projective, the quotient map
B_{p}\twoheadrightarrow G_{p}C
has
a right inverse. So
G_{p}C
is a direct summand of
B_{p}
; hence

(B_{p})^{*}/(G_{p}C)^{*}
is one of
(B_{p})^{*}
, so is projective. But
(B_{p})^{*}=:(B^{\dagger})_{-p}
. And
(G_{p}C)^{*}=G_{-p}(C^{*})
by
(
2.7
)(3). Also,
(B^{\dagger})_{-p}/G_{-p}(C^{*})=G_{-p}(B^{\dagger}/C^{*})
by (
2.5
). Thus
G_{-p}(B^{\dagger}/C^{*})

is projective, so flat; hence,
G_{\bullet}(B^{\dagger}/C^{*}):=\bigoplus G_{-p}(B^{\dagger}/C^{*})
is flat too. Thus
C^{*}\in\mathbf{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
.

Conversely, given
D\in\mathbf{F}\Delta_{B^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
, note

D^{*}\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}}
by (
2.7
)(4). The first assertion of
(2) now follows from (1), and the second, from (
3.3
).

Plainly, if
I
and
D
are homogeneous, then so are
(0:_{B^{\dagger}}I)
and

(0:_{B}D)
. Thus the second correspondence restricts as asserted.
Finally, (
3.3
) now yields the last two assertions of (2). Thus
(2) holds.
∎

(3.6) Remark.

If
B\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
, but
A
and
B
are not graded, then (
3.5
)
is generalized by the statement below. Its proof is omitted, as it is
similar and simpler. If
A
and
B
are graded, then the statement
below is a corollary of (
3.5
).

First, some notation. Denote by
\mathbf{Q}_{B}
the set of quotient

A
-modules
C
of
B
such that
C\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. If
A
is filtered
and
B\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
, denote by
\mathbf{FQ}_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
the subset of

C\in\mathbf{Q}_{B}
such that, with the induced filtration,

C\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}}
.

Denote by
\mathbf{S}_{B}
the set of
A
-submodules
D
of
B
with
B/D\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. If
A
is filtered and
B\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
, denote by
\mathbf{FS}_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
the subset of
D\in\mathbf{S}_{B}
such that, with the induced
filtrations,
D\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}}
and
B/D\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
.

If
A
is graded and
B\in\mathbb{AG}_{A}
, denote by
\mathbf{HQ}_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}

and
\mathbf{HS}_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
the analogues of
\mathbf{FQ}_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
and
\mathbf{FS}_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
where
C
and
D
are homogeneous.

Here’s the statement in the corresponding two parts:

(1)
Then
C\mapsto C^{*}
gives a bijection
\mathbf{Q}_{B}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\mathbf{S}_{B^{*}}
,
and
D\mapsto D^{*}
, its inverse.

(2)
Assume
A
is filtered and
B\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
. Then there’s a
commutative square:

\kern 17.46417pt\kern-11.21922pt\mathbf{FQ}_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to34.41331pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits^{\sim}$}\mathbf{FS}_{B^{*}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle$\hss}}

\kern 17.46417pt\kern-17.46417pt\mathbf{HQ}_{G_{\bullet}B}^{\mathbf{h}}\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to22.08315pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits^{\sim}$}\mathbf{HS}_{G_{\bullet}B^{*}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}

The horizontal maps are, as indicated, bijective; they’re given by

C\mapsto C^{*}
, and their inverses, by
D\mapsto D^{*}
. The vertical
maps are given by
C\mapsto G_{\bullet}C
and
D\mapsto G_{\bullet}D
.

(3.7) Example.

Fix variables
X_{1},\dotsc,X_{r}
; take
A:=k[X_{1},\dotsc,X_{r}]
. Weight

X_{i}
by a positive integer
w_{i}
; grade the polynomial ring
A
by
weighted degree. Thus the graded piece
A_{n}
is the free
k
-module on
the monomials
M
of weighted degree
n
.

The graded dual
A^{\dagger}
can be usefully viewed as the free

k
-module on all the Laurent monomials
1/M
, namely as

k[X_{1}^{-1},\dotsc,X_{r}^{-1}]
, provided the product of a monomial
L\in A
with
1/M
is taken to be
L/M
if
L\mid M
and to be
0
if not.
Then the graded piece
(A^{\dagger})_{-n}
is the free
k
-module on the

1/M
of weighted degree
-n
.

As an abstract
A
-module,
A^{\dagger}
is, plainly, independent of the
choice of the
w_{i}
. Moreover (cf.
[
KES
]
*Subsec. 3.2),
A^{\dagger}

consists of the maps
A\to k
that are continuous when
k
is given the
discrete topology and
A
is given the topology defined by the
filtration with
F^{p}A
generated by the monomials of degree at least

p
; notice that two choices of the
w_{i}
give rise to cofinal
filtrations, and so the same topology on
A
. Thus, a better name for

A^{\dagger}
would be the
continuous dual
.

If
w_{i}=1
for all
i
, then
A^{\dagger}
is equal to the
k
-algebra of
divided powers; see
[
Eis
]
*Sec. A2.4 or
[
JPAA217
]
*Sec. 3,
pp. 2258–59. If
k=\mathbb{C}
too, then
A^{\dagger}
is the usual algebra of
polynomial differential operators on
A
; see
[
Eis
]
*Prp. A2.8.
Remarkably, in this case,
A
can as well be viewed as the algebra of
polynomial differential operators on
A^{\dagger}
, viewed as a polynomial
ring in the
X_{i}^{-1}
; see
[
Eis
]
*Ex. 21.7. These additional
structures certainly have their charm (and in (
3.4
), justify the
term “apolarity”), but in the present work, they’d just add an
unnecessary layer of complication.

In (
3.5
)(2), take
B:=A
. Note that an
A
-submodule
D
of

B^{\dagger}
is finitely generated over
A
iff it’s finitely generated over

k
. Also, note that, if
0\neq C:=A/I
, then
I\subset F^{1}(A):=\langle X_{1},\dotsc,X_{r}\rangle
(and conversely) because
G_{0}C=k
since
G_{0}A\to G_{0}C
is surjective,
G_{0}A=k
, and
G_{0}C
is locally free and is
nonzero as
1\in G_{0}C
and
G_{\bullet}C
is of the same rank as
C
, so is
nonzero. Thus (
3.5
)(2) recovers Macaulay Duality essentially
as is stated in
[
Eis
]
*Thm. 21.6, but
k
is any Noetherian ring
in (
3.5
)(2), not just a field, and the weights
w_{i}
needn’t be
1. In addition, (
3.5
)(2) asserts the compatibility of duality
with forming associated graded modules.

Finally, let
\varphi\colon C\to A^{*}
be an
A
-map. Say
A_{n}\subset I
for
n>n_{0}
. Then, given any monomial
M\in A_{n}
with
n>n_{0}
, note
M\cdot C=0
; hence,
M\cdot\varphi(C)=0
. Now, given any
c\in C
, say
\varphi(c)=\prod_{n\geq 0}\bigl(\bigoplus_{i}(a_{in}/N_{in})\bigr)
where

a_{in}\in k
and
N_{in}\in A_{n}
. If
a_{jp}\neq 0
, then
N_{jp}\cdot\varphi(c)\neq 0
, and so
p\leq n_{o}
. Thus
\varphi(c)\in\bigoplus_{n=0}^{n_{0}}(A_{n})^{*}
. Thus
\varphi(C)\subset A^{\dagger}
. Thus

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A^{*})=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A^{\dagger})
. But
C^{*}=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A^{*})
by
(
2.2.3
). Thus
C^{*}=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A^{\dagger})
, as shown in a
different way assuming
k
is a field in the course of the proof of

[
Eis
]
*Thm. 21.6, p. 531.

4.
The
k
-socle

(4.1) Setup.

Keep the setup of (
3.1
)

Fix a filtered
k
-algebra
A
with
A/F^{1}A=k
; that is, the
composition
k\to A\to k
is the identity.
Fix a finite function
\mathbf{h}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
, and
C\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}^{\mathbf{h}}
.
Set

i:=i(\mathbf{h})s:=s(\mathbf{h})t:=\mathbf{h}(s).

Note
C\big/((F^{1}A)\cdot C)=C\otimes_{A}k
, and
C\otimes_{A}k
has the
filtration induced from
C
.

Given a
k
-algebra
K
, note that
C_{K}
carries an induced filtration;
by definition,
F^{n}(C_{K})
is the image of
(F^{n}C)_{K}
in
C_{K}
.

(4.2) Definition.

The
k
-socle
of
C
and its induced filtration are given by

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C):=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(k,\,C)=\{\,c\in C\mid(F^{1}A)\cdot c=0\,\}=:(0:_{C}F^{1}A)\subset C,

\displaystyle F^{n}(\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)):=F^{n}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(k,\,C)=\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)\bigcap F^{n}C\quad\text{for all }n.

(4.3) Lemma.

In
\mathbb{F}_{A}
, there’s a canonical isomorphism:
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)=(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)^{*}
.

Proof.

Taking
B:=k
in (
3.2.1
) and taking
B:=C^{*}
and
C:=k

in (
2.2.2
) yield

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(k,\,C)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C^{*},\,k^{*})=(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)^{*}\text{\quad in\kern 5.0pt}\mathbb{F}_{A}.

Thus (
4.2
) yields the assertion.
∎

(4.4) Lemma.

(1)
Then
G_{i}C\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}G_{i}(C\otimes_{A}k)
.
(2)
Then
G_{s}(\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C))=G_{s}C
.

(3)
Choose a
k
-submodule
N\subset C
that maps isomorphically onto

G_{i}C
(such an
N
exists because
G_{i}C
is projective). Then
A\cdot N=C
iff
C\otimes_{A}k=G_{i}C
.

(4)
Then
(a)

\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)=G_{s}C
and
C^{*}\otimes_{A}k\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
iff

(b)

C^{*}\otimes_{A}k=G_{-s}(C^{*})
.

Proof.

For (1),
recall
C\otimes_{A}k=C/(F^{1}A)C
. Let
\kappa\colon C\twoheadrightarrow C\otimes_{A}k
be the quotient map. Then
F^{n}(C\otimes_{A}k):=\kappa(F^{n}C)
for
all
n
; so
\kappa^{-1}(F^{n}(C\otimes_{A}k))=F^{n}C+\mathop{\rm Ker}(\kappa)
. But

\mathop{\rm Ker}(\kappa)=(F^{1}A)C
and
(F^{1}A)C\subset F^{1+i}C
. So

\kappa^{-1}(F^{1+i}(C\otimes_{A}k))=F^{1+i}C
. Thus
C/(F^{1+i}C)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}(C\otimes_{A}k)/(F^{1+i}(C\otimes_{A}k))
. Thus (1) holds.

For (2),
note
F^{s+1}C=0
. So
F^{s+1}(\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C))=0
. But

(F^{1}A)(F^{s}C)\subset F^{1+s}C
. So
F^{s}C\subset\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)
. So
F^{s}C=F^{s}(\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C))
. Thus (2) holds.

For (3),
set
B:=A/F^{s-i+1}A
. Then
C
is a
B
-module. Set
\mathfrak{N}:=F^{1}B
. Then
\mathfrak{N}^{s-i+1}=0
, and
B/\mathfrak{N}=k
. So by
Nakayama’s lemma,
B\cdot N=C
iff
(B\cdot N)\otimes_{B}k
maps onto

C\otimes_{B}k
, iff
N\to C\otimes_{B}k
is surjective. However,
B\cdot N=A\cdot N
and
C\otimes_{B}k=C\otimes_{A}k
. Thus
A\cdot N=C
iff
N\to C\otimes_{A}k
is surjective.

By hypothesis,
N\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}G_{i}C
. But
G_{i}C=G_{i}(C\otimes_{A}k)
by (1). So

N\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}G_{i}(C\otimes_{A}k)
. The latter factors through
C\otimes_{A}k
. So

N\to C\otimes_{A}k
is injective; so surjective iff it’s an isomorphism; so
iff
C\otimes_{A}k\to G_{i}C
is an isomorphism. Thus (3) holds.

For (4),
first assume (a). Then
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)^{*}=(G_{s}C)^{*}
and

(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)^{**}=C^{*}\otimes_{A}k
. Thus (
4.3
) and
(
2.7
)(3) yield (b). Conversely, assume (b). Then
C^{*}\otimes_{A}k\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
as
G_{-s}(C^{*})\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
by (
2.7
)(4).
Also,
(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)^{*}=(G_{-s}(C^{*}))^{*}
. So (
4.3
) and
(
2.7
)(3) yield
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)=((G_{s}C)^{*})^{*}=G_{s}C
. Thus (a)
holds. Thus (4) holds.
∎

(4.5) Definition.

Fix a finite function
\mathbf{t}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
.

Call
\mathbf{t}
the
local generator type
of
C
if
C\otimes_{A}k
is in

\mathbb{AF}_{A}
with Hilbert function
\mathbf{t}
.

Call
\mathbf{t}
the
k
-socle type
of
C
if
C^{*}
has local

generator type
\mathbf{t}^{*}
. (Then
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)
is in
\mathbb{AF}_{A}
and has
Hilbert function
\mathbf{t}
by (
4.3
) and (
2.7
)(4), but
perhaps not conversely.)

Call
C

level
if
C^{*}\otimes_{A}k=G_{-s}(C^{*})
, or equivalently by

(
4.4
)(4), if
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)=G_{s}C
and
C^{*}\otimes_{A}k\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. If
C
is graded, then
C
is level iff
(C^{*})_{-s}

generates
C^{*}
by (
4.4
)(3). If
C
is level, call
s
its

k
-socle degree
and
t
its
type
.

(4.6) Lemma.

There’s a canonical commutative diagram of surjective maps:

\kern 16.34862pt\kern-16.34862ptG_{\bullet}(C^{*})\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to24.41771pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits^{\kappa_{1}}$}G_{\bullet}(C^{*})\otimes_{G_{\bullet}A}k\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to24.47903pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits^{\lambda_{1}}$}G_{-s}(C^{*})

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle=$\hss}}

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\mu$\hss}}

\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$}}\Big\downarrow\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle=$\hss}}

\kern 16.34862pt\kern-16.34862ptG_{\bullet}(C^{*})\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to30.73207pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits^{\kappa_{2}}$}G_{\bullet}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)\hbox{$\mathop{\hbox to30.7934pt{\kern 3.75pt\rightarrowfill\kern 3.75pt}}\limits^{\lambda_{2}}$}G_{-s}(C^{*})

(4.6.1)

Proof.

Plainly, in
G_{\bullet}(C^{*})
, there’s this nested sequence of ideals:

\textstyle\bigl(\bigoplus_{j>0}G_{j}A\bigr)\cdot G_{\bullet}(C^{*})\subset G_{\bullet}\bigl((F^{1}A)\cdot C^{*}\bigr)\subset\bigoplus_{j>-s}G_{j}(C^{*}).

A few applications of Noether’s Third Isomorphism Theorem now yields
(
4.6.1
).
∎

(4.7) Proposition.

Assume
G_{\bullet}C
is level. Then so is
C
.

Proof.

Consider(
4.6.1
). As
G_{\bullet}C
is level,
\lambda_{1}
is
bijective. So
\mu
is injective. But
\mu
is surjective. So
\mu

is bijective. So
\lambda_{2}
is bijective. So
G_{j}(C^{*}\otimes k)=0
for

j\neq-s
by (
4.4
)(1) with
C^{*}
for
C
. So
G_{\bullet}(C^{*}\otimes k)=C^{*}\otimes k
. Thus
C^{*}\otimes k=G_{-s}(C^{*})
; that is,
C
is level.
∎

(4.8) Lemma.

Let
V
and
W
be
k
-modules, with
V
finitely generated.

(1)
Let
K
be a
k
-algebra. Assume
V
is flat. Then
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(V,W)_{K}=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(V_{K},W_{K})
and
(V^{*})_{K}=(V_{K})^{*}
.

(2)
Assume that, for every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
k
, there’s a
field
K
containing
k/\mathfrak{m}
such that
V_{K}=0
. Then
V=0
.

(3)
Let
\varphi\colon V\to W
be a map. Assume
W
is flat and finitely
generated. Assume, for every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
k
, there’s a
field
K
containing
k/\mathfrak{m}
such that the induced map
\varphi_{K}\colon V_{K}\to W_{K}
is an isomorphism. Then
\varphi
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

In (1),
as
V
is locally free of finite rank,
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(V,W)_{K}\to\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(V_{K},W_{K})
is an isomorphism. And so, taking
W:=k
yields
(V^{*})_{K}=(V_{K})^{*}
.

For (2),
note
V=0
if, for every
\mathfrak{m}
, the localization
V_{\mathfrak{m}}
is 0. By Nakayama’s Lemma,
V_{\mathfrak{m}}=0
if
V_{\mathfrak{m}}\otimes_{k_{\mathfrak{m}}}(k_{\mathfrak{m}}/\mathfrak{m}k_{\mathfrak{m}})=0
; so, if
V\otimes_{k}(k/\mathfrak{m})=0
;
so, if
V\otimes_{k}K=0
. Thus (2) holds.

For (3),
set
Q:=\mathop{\rm Cok}\varphi
. Then
V_{K}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{K}}W_{K}\to Q_{K}\to 0
is
exact for every
K
. But
\varphi_{K}
is an isomorphism. So
Q_{K}=0
. But

W
is finitely generated; so
Q
is too. Thus (2) yields
Q=0
.

Set
P:=\mathop{\rm Ker}\varphi
. As
W
is flat,
0\to P_{K}\to V_{K}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{K}}W_{K}\to 0
is exact for every
K
. But
\varphi_{K}
is an isomorphism. So
P_{K}=0
. But
V
is finitely generated; so
P
is too. So
P=0
by (2).
Thus (3) holds.
∎

(4.9) Lemma.

Let
B
and
K
be
k
-algebras, and
M
and
N
two
B
-modules. Then

(M\otimes_{B}N)_{K}=(M_{K})\otimes_{B_{K}}(N_{K}).

(4.9.1)

Proof.

By Associativity,
(M\otimes_{B}N)_{K}=M\otimes_{B}N_{K}
. But
N_{K}
is a

B_{K}
-module, and it’s an
B
-module via restriction of scalars. So

M\otimes_{B}N_{K}=(M\otimes_{B}B_{K})\otimes_{B_{K}}N_{K}
by Cancellation. But
M\otimes_{B}B_{K}=M_{K}
also by Cancellation. Thus (
4.9.1
) holds.
∎

(4.10) Lemma.

Let
K
be a
k
-algebra, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(1)
Then the defining surjection is an isomorphism:
(F^{n}C)_{K}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}F^{n}(C_{K})
.

(2)
Then there’s a canonical isomorphism:
G_{n}(C_{K})=(G_{n}C)_{K}
.

(3)
Then the conditions of (
4.1
) hold for
A_{K}
and
C_{K}
in
place of
A
and
C
.

(4)
Then there are canonical isomorphisms of filtered modules:

\displaystyle(C^{*})_{K}

\displaystyle=(C_{K})^{*}

(4.10.1)

\displaystyle(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)_{K}

\displaystyle=(C_{K})^{*}\otimes_{A_{K}}K

(4.10.2)

Proof.

For (1),
tensor the sequence
0\to F^{n+1}C\to F^{n}C\to G_{n}C\to 0
with
K
. The resulting sequence is exact, because
G_{n}C
is flat.
So
(F^{n+1}C)_{K}\subset(F^{n}C)_{K}
and

(F^{n}C)_{K}\big/(F^{n+1}C)_{K}=(G_{n}C)_{K}.

(4.10.3)

But
F^{n}C=C
for
n\ll 0
. So
(F^{n}C)_{K}\subset C_{K}
for all

n
. Thus (1) holds.

Note (2)
follows from (
4.10.3
) and (1).

For (3),
tensor
0\to F^{1}A\to A\to k\to 0
with
K
, getting an exact
sequence. So
(F^{1}A)_{K}=F^{1}(A_{K})
and
A_{K}\big/(F^{1}A)_{K}=K
, as
desired.

Recall
G_{n}C
is locally free of constant rank
\mathbf{h}(n)
over
k
;
hence,
G_{n}(C_{K})
is so over
K
by (2). Hence
C_{K}
is in

\mathbb{AF}_{A_{K}}
and has Hilbert function
\mathbf{h}
. Thus (3) holds.

For (4),
note (
4.10.1
) results from these canonical
isomorphisms, justified after:

\displaystyle F^{n}((C^{*})_{K})

\displaystyle=(F^{n}(C^{*}))_{K}

(4.10.4)

\displaystyle=((C/F^{1-n}C)^{*})_{K}

(4.10.5)

\displaystyle=((C/F^{1-n}C)_{K})^{*}

(4.10.6)

\displaystyle=(C_{K}\big/(F^{1-n}C)_{K})^{*}

(4.10.7)

\displaystyle=(C_{K}\big/F^{1-n}(C_{K}))^{*}

(4.10.8)

\displaystyle=F^{n}((C_{K})^{*}).

(4.10.9)

First, (
4.10.4
) holds by (1), as
C^{*}\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
by
(
2.7
)(4). Next, (
4.10.5
) and (
4.10.9
) hold
by (
2.2.1
). Next,
C/F^{1-n}C
is locally free of finite rank by
(
2.7
)(1); so (
4.8
)(1) yields (
4.10.6
). Next,
(
4.10.7
) holds by the right exactness of
\bullet\otimes_{k}K
.
Finally, (
4.10.8
) holds by (1). Thus (
4.10.1
)
holds.

Take
B:=A
and
N:=k
in (
4.9.1
). Note
N_{K}=k_{K}
where

k
is viewed as a
k
-module via the composition
k\to A\to k
. But
that’s the identity. Thus
N_{K}=K
.

Take
M:=C^{*}
also. Then (
4.9.1
) becomes
(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)_{K}=(C^{*})_{K}\otimes_{A_{K}}K
. This canonical isomorphism preserves the
filtrations, because the
n
th term on each side is, by definition, the
image of
(F^{n}(C^{*}))_{K}
. Thus (
4.10.1
) now yields
(
4.10.2
).
∎

(4.11) Proposition.

Given a function
\mathbf{t}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
, these conditions are equivalent:

(1)

The
A
-module
C
has
k
-socle type
\mathbf{t}
.

(2)

For every
k
-algebra
K
, the
A_{K}
-module
C_{K}
has
K
-socle
type
\mathbf{t}
.

(3)

(a)

The
k
-module
G_{n}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)
is flat for
-s<n\leq-i
.

(b)

For every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
k
, there’s a field
K

containing
k/\mathfrak{m}
such that
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{K}(C_{K})
has Hilbert function

\mathbf{t}
.

Proof.

Assume (1). By (
4.10
)(3), the conditions of (
4.1
)
hold for the pair
A_{K},\,C_{K}
and also, owing to (1), for the pair

A_{K},\,(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)_{K}
with
\mathbf{t}^{*}
for
\mathbf{h}
. Hence,
(
4.10.2
) implies that
(C_{K})^{*}\otimes_{A_{K}}K
is in

\mathbb{AF}_{A_{K}}
and has Hilbert function
\mathbf{t}^{*}
. Thus (2) holds.

Assume (2). Then (3) holds as it’s a special case of (2).

Assume (3). Fix
n
. Note
C^{*}\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
with Hilbert polynomial

\mathbf{h}^{*}
by (
2.7
)(4). So (
2.7
)(1) with
C^{*}
for
C

implies
C^{*}
is finitely generated over
k
. So
C^{*}\otimes_{A}k
is too.
Thus
G_{n}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)
is.

If
n\leq-s
, then
F^{n}(C^{*})=C^{*}
, so
F^{n}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)=C^{*}\otimes_{A}k
. If

n>-i
. then
F^{n}(C^{*})=0
, so
F^{n}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)=0
. Thus

G_{n}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)=0
if
n<-s
or
n>-i
.

However,
G_{-s}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)=G_{-s}(C^{*})
by (
4.4
)(1); so

G_{-s}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)
is flat. Thus (a) implies that, whatever
n
is,

G_{n}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)
is flat, so locally free of finite rank. It remains to
show that
G_{n}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)
has constant rank
\mathbf{t}^{*}(n)
.

So given any
\mathfrak{m}
and
K
as in (b), we have to see

\dim_{K}((G_{n}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k))_{K})=\mathbf{t}^{*}(n)
. But
(G_{n}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k))_{K}=G_{n}((C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)_{K})
by the proof of (
4.10
)(2) with

C^{*}\otimes_{A}k
for
C
, as that proof doesn’t require the rank to be
constant.

So by (
4.10.2
), we have to see
(C_{K})^{*}\otimes_{A_{K}}K
has Hilbert
function
\mathbf{t}^{*}
, or by (
2.7
)(3), equivalently,

((C_{K})^{*}\otimes_{A_{K}}K)^{*}
has Hilbert function
\mathbf{t}
. Apply
(
4.3
) with
C_{K}
for
C
, noting
C_{K}\in\mathbb{AF}_{A_{K}}
as
K

is a field. Thus (b) now yields (1).
∎

(4.12) Corollary.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

The
A
-module
C
is level of type
t
.

(2)

For every
k
-algebra
K
, the
A_{K}
-module
C_{K}
is level of type

t
.

(3)

For every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
k
, there is a field
K

containing
k/\mathfrak{m}
such that

\dim_{K}(\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{K}(C_{K}))=t.

Proof.

Define
\mathbf{t}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
by
\mathbf{t}(s):=t
and
\mathbf{t}(j):=0
for

j\neq s
. Then (
4.12
) results from (
4.11
), because
(
4.12
)(3) implies
G_{n}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)=0
for
-s<n
, as shown
next.

We have to show
F^{1-s}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)=0
, or equivalently,
C^{*}\otimes_{A}k\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}G_{-s}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)
. But
G_{-s}(C^{*}\otimes_{A}k)=G_{-s}(C^{*})
by
(
4.4
)(1) with
C^{*}
for
C
. Thus there’s a canonical surjection

\varphi\colon C^{*}\otimes_{A}k\twoheadrightarrow G_{-s}(C^{*})
, and we have to show it’s an
isomorphism.

So given any
\mathfrak{m}
and
K
as in (3), it suffices by (
4.8
)(3)
to show
\varphi\otimes_{k}K
is an isomorphism, as
G_{-s}(C^{*})
is projective.
Since
\varphi\otimes_{k}K
is surjective and
K
is a field, it suffices to show
its source and target have the same dimension over
K
.

The source is equal to
(C_{K})^{*}\otimes_{A_{K}}K
by (
4.10.2
). Its
dimension is equal to that of
((C_{K})^{*}\otimes_{A_{K}}K)^{*}
, as
K
is a
field; so to that of
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{K}(C_{K})
by (
4.3
); so to
t
by (3).
Finally, the target is
(G_{-s}(C^{*}))_{K}
, and
G_{-s}(C^{*})
is locally
free of rank
\mathbf{h}^{*}(-s)
by (
2.7
)(4); so the dimension of the
target is
\mathbf{h}(s)=:t
, as desired.
∎

(4.13)  (Multilevel modules).

First, assume
A
and
C
are graded. Set
D:=C^{*}
,

\textstyle\Delta^{m}D:=A\bigl(\bigoplus_{j=m}^{s}D_{-j}\bigr)\subset D\Lambda^{m}C:=(\Delta^{m}D)^{*}\quad\text{for all }m.

(4.13.1)

Fix
\mathbf{h}_{m}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
for all
m
. If
\Delta^{m}D\in\mathbf{H\Delta}_{D}^{\mathbf{h}_{m}^{*}}
for all
m
, call
C

multilevel

and the
\mathbf{h}_{m}

its Hilbert functions
.

Given such a
C
, note each
\Lambda^{m}C
is a flat quotient of
C
.
Moreover,
\Delta^{m}D=D
for
m\leq i
, and
\Delta^{m}D=0
for
m>s
;
so
\mathbf{h}_{m}=\mathbf{h}
for
m\leq i
, and
\mathbf{h}_{m}=0
for
m>s
. Also,

C
is level iff
h_{m}=h
for
m\leq s
, iff
\Lambda^{m}C=C
for
m\leq s
. Lastly, for all
m
and
p\geq m
, plainly
(\Lambda^{m}C)_{p}=C_{p}
,
and so
\mathbf{h}_{m}(p)=\mathbf{h}(p)
.

Next, assume
C
is filtered. If the associated graded module
G_{\bullet}C

is multilevel (but not necessarily of the same
k
-socle type as
C
), call

C

multilevel
. And if the
\mathbf{h}_{m}
are the Hilbert functions
of
G_{\bullet}C
, call them the
Hilbert functions
of
C
as well.

Notice that, if
C
is level filtered,
C
need not be multilevel, as

G_{\bullet}C
may be of a different
k
-socle type. For example, below, the
algebra
C
in (
5.5
) is
k
-Gorenstein, so level of type 1 by
(
5.3
), but
G_{\bullet}C
isn’t
k
-Gorenstein when
C
is filtered
via the standard weighting, although
G_{\bullet}C
is
k
-Gorenstein via a
different weighting.

Finally, given a graded
A
-module
B
, denote the sets of all
multilevel homogeneous and of all multilevel filtered quotients of
B

by
\mathbf{H\Lambda}_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{n}\}}
and by
\mathbf{F\Lambda}_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{n}\}}
.

(4.14) Proposition.

In the setup of (
4.13
), fix
C\in\mathbf{H\Lambda}_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{n}\}}
, fix a
k
-algebra
K
, and fix an integer
n
. For all
p
, set

\mathbf{t}(p):=\mathbf{h}_{p}(p)-\mathbf{h}_{p+1}(p)
.

(1)
Then
C_{K}\in\mathbf{H\Lambda}_{B_{K}}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{n}\}}
and

\Lambda^{m}(C_{K})=(\Lambda^{m}C)_{K}
and
\Delta^{m}(C^{*}_{K})=(\Delta^{m}C^{*})_{K}
.

(2)
Then
\mathbf{t}
is the
k
-socle type of
C
.

(3)
Set
\mathbf{h}^{\diamond}_{m}:=\mathbf{h}_{n}
for
m\leq n
and
\mathbf{h}^{\diamond}_{m}:=\mathbf{h}_{m}

for
m\geq n
. Then
\Lambda^{n}C\in\mathbf{H\Lambda}_{B_{K}}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\diamond}_{n}\}}
.

(4)
Assume
\mathbf{t}(n-1)\neq 0
, and set
\overline{C}:=\bigl(\Delta^{n-1}D\big/\Delta^{n}D\bigr)^{*}
and
\overline{\mathbf{h}}:=\mathbf{h}_{n-1}-\mathbf{h}_{n}
.
Then
\overline{C}
is level, and
\overline{\mathbf{h}}
is its Hilbert function.

Proof.

In (1),
the assertions are easy to check.

For (2),
by (
4.5
), we have to show that
D_{-p}\big/(F^{1}A\cdot D)_{-p}
is locally free of rank
\mathbf{t}(p)
. But
(F^{1}A\cdot D)_{-p}=(\Delta^{p+1}D)_{-p}
, and
\Delta^{p+1}D\in\mathbf{H\Delta}_{D}^{\mathbf{h}_{p+1}^{*}}
. Thus (2) holds.

For (3),
note
\Delta^{m}\Delta^{n}D=\Delta^{n}D
for
m\leq n
and

\Delta^{m}\Delta^{n}D=\Delta^{m}D
for
m\geq n
. For
m\geq n
, form the
following exact sequence:

0\to\Delta^{n}D\big/\Delta^{m}D\to D\big/\Delta^{m}D\to D\big/\Delta^{n}D\to 0.

(4.14.1)

For all
p
, recall
\Delta^{p}D\in\smash{\mathbf{H\Delta}}_{D}^{\mathbf{h}_{p}^{*}}
. Hence
\Delta^{m}\Delta^{n}D\in\smash{\mathbf{H\Delta}}_{\Delta^{n}D}^{\mathbf{h}^{\diamond*}_{m}}
. Thus (3) holds.

For (4),
note
\overline{C}\in\mathbb{AG}_{A}^{\overline{\mathbf{h}}}
by (
4.14.1
) with

n-1
for
n
and with
n
for
m
. However, plainly,

(\Delta^{n-1}D)_{m}=(F^{1}A\cdot\Delta^{n-2}D)_{m}
for
m>-n+1
, and

(\overline{C}^{*})_{m}=0
for
m<-n+1
; so
\overline{C}^{*}\otimes k=(\overline{C}^{*})_{-n+1}
. Thus
(4) holds.
∎

(4.15) Proposition.

Keep the setup of (
4.13
), and fix
n
. Set
\overline{C}:=\bigl(D\big/\Delta^{n}D\bigr)^{*}
and
\overline{\mathbf{h}}:=\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}_{n}
. Set
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{m}:=\mathbf{h}_{n}
for
m\leq n
and
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{m}:=\mathbf{h}_{m}
for
m\geq n
.
Assume
\overline{C}
is level, and
\overline{\mathbf{h}}
is its Hilbert function. Assume

\Lambda^{n}C\in\mathbf{H\Lambda}_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{n}\}}
and
\mathbf{h}_{m}=\mathbf{h}
for
m<n
. Then
C\in\mathbf{H\Lambda}_{B}^{\{\mathbf{h}_{n}\}}
.

Proof.

We have to show
\Delta^{m}D\in\mathbf{H\Delta}_{D}^{\mathbf{h}_{m}^{*}}
for all
m
.

Assume
m<n
. Set
\overline{D}:=D/\Delta^{n}D=\overline{C}^{*}
. Since
\overline{C}
is
level,
\Delta^{m}\overline{D}=\overline{D}
. But
\Delta^{m}\overline{D}=\Delta^{m}D/\Delta^{n}D
.
Hence
\Delta^{m}D=D
. By hypothesis,
\mathbf{h}_{m}=\mathbf{h}
; so
\mathbf{h}_{m}^{*}=\mathbf{h}^{*}
. Thus
\Delta^{m}D\in\mathbf{H\Delta}_{D}^{\mathbf{h}_{m}^{*}}
.

Assume
m\geq n
. Then
\Delta^{m}\Delta^{n}D=\Delta^{m}D
. But

\Lambda^{n}C
is multilevel, and the
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}_{m}
, its Hilbert functions.
Thus
\Delta^{n}D\big/\Delta^{m}D
is flat, and
\Delta^{m}D
has
\mathbf{h}_{m}^{*}
as Hilbert function.

As
\overline{C}
is level,
D\big/\Delta^{n}D
is flat. But (
4.14.1
) is
exact. Thus
D/\Delta^{m}D
is flat.
∎

5.
k
-Gorenstein algebras

(5.1) Setup.

Keep the setup of (
3.1
). In addition, recall from (
2.6
),
that we call
A

k-Gorenstein
if
A^{*}
is a locally free of

rank 1 (that is, invertible).

(5.2) Proposition.

Let
A
be a
k
-Artinian
k
-algebra. Then these are equivalent:

(1)

The
k
-algebra
A
is
k
-Gorenstein.

(2)

For every
k
-algebra
K
, the
K
-algebra
A_{K}
is

K
-Gorenstein.

(3)

For every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
k
, there is a field
K

containing
k/\mathfrak{m}
such that the
K
-algebra
A_{K}
is
K
-Gorenstein.

(4)

For every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
k
, there is a field
K

containing
k/\mathfrak{m}
such that the
A_{K}
-module
(A^{*})_{K}
is cyclic.

Proof.

Assume (1). Then
(A^{*})_{K}
is an invertible
A_{K}
-module for every

k
-algebra
K
. But
(A^{*})_{K}=(A_{K})^{*}
by (
4.8
)(1), and
plainly,
A_{K}
is
K
-Artinian. Thus (2) holds.

Trivially, (2) implies (3).

Assume (3). Then, given
K
as in (3), the
A_{K}
-module
(A_{K})^{*}
is
invertible. But
A_{K}
is semilocal. Hence,
(A_{K})^{*}
is free of rank
1; so, cyclic. But
(A^{*})_{K}=(A_{K})^{*}
by
4.8
(1). Thus (4)
holds.

Assume (4). Given
\mathfrak{m}
, set
L:=k/\mathfrak{m}
. Below we find an
f\in A^{*}
whose image in
(A^{*})_{L}
generates it over
A_{L}
. Given
f
,
define
\varphi\colon A\to A^{*}
by
\alpha\mapsto\alpha f
. Then the induced

A_{L}
-map
\varphi_{L}\colon A_{L}\to(A^{*})_{L}
is surjective. But
A
and
A^{*}
are
locally free of the same rank at
\mathfrak{m}
. So
A_{L}
and
(A^{*})_{L}
have
the same
L
-dimension. So
\varphi_{L}
is an isomorphism. So the
localization
\varphi_{\mathfrak{m}}
is an isomorphism by (
4.8
)(3). Thus
(1) holds.

To find
f
, let
\overline{K}
be the algebraic closure of
K
. By
Cancellation,
A_{\overline{K}}=A_{K}\otimes_{K}\overline{K}
and
(A^{*})_{\overline{K}}=(A^{*})_{K}\otimes_{K}\overline{K}
. Thus
(A^{*})_{\overline{K}}
is a cyclic
A_{\overline{K}}
-module.

Note
A_{L}
is a finite product of Artinian local
L
-algebras
A_{i}
. In
turn, each
A_{i}\otimes_{L}\overline{K}
is a finite product of Artinian local

\overline{K}
-algebras
B_{ij}
. As
\overline{K}
is algebraically closed, the residue
field of each
B_{ij}
is
\overline{K}
.

Note
A^{*}\otimes_{A}B_{ij}
is a quotient of
(A^{*}\otimes_{A}A_{i})\otimes_{L}\overline{K}
, which is
a quotient of
(A^{*})_{\overline{K}}
. So
A^{*}\otimes_{A}B_{ij}
is a cyclic

B_{ij}
-module. So
(A^{*}\otimes_{A}B_{ij})\otimes_{B_{ij}}\overline{K}
is a
1-dimensional
\overline{K}
-vector space.

Fix
i
and
j
. Take
f_{i}\in A^{*}\otimes_{A}A_{i}
whose image in

(A^{*}\otimes_{A}B_{ij})\otimes_{B_{ij}}\overline{K}
is nonzero, so generates it. By
Nakayama’s Lemma, the image of
f_{i}
in
A^{*}\otimes_{A}B_{ij}
generates it.

Note
A_{i}\otimes_{L}\overline{K}
is flat over
A_{i}
. Hence
B_{ij}
is flat over

A_{i}
, so faithfully flat since these rings are local. Hence
f_{i}

generates
A^{*}\otimes_{A}A_{i}
over
A_{i}
, as its image generates

A^{*}\otimes_{A}B_{ij}
over
B_{ij}
. So
\prod f_{i}
generates
(A^{*})_{L}
over

A_{L}
. Lift
\prod f_{i}
to some
f\in A^{*}
. Then
f
is the desired
element.
∎

(5.3) Proposition.

Assume
A
is
k
-Artinian with (finite) Hilbert function
\mathbf{h}
.

(A)
Then these five conditions are equivalent:

(1)

The
k
-algebra
A
is
k
-Gorenstein (namely, the
A
-module

A^{*}
is invertible).

(2)

The
k
-module
A^{*}\otimes_{A}k
is invertible.

(3)

The
k
-module
A^{*}\otimes_{A}k
is flat, and the
k
-module

\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(A)
is invertible.

(4)

The
A
-module
A
is level of type
1
.

(5)

In
\mathbb{M}_{A}
, there’s an isomorphism
A\cong L\otimes_{k}A^{*}
for
some
k
-module
L
.

Moreover, if
(1)
–
(5)
hold, then
L
is invertible, and
L\cong\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(A)=G_{s}A
.

(B)
If
A\in\mathbb{AG}_{A}
, say
A=\bigoplus A_{n}
, then
(1)
–
(5)
are
equivalent to this condition:

(6)

In
\mathbb{AG}_{A}
, there’s a canonical (homogeneous)
isomorphism
A=A_{s}\otimes_{k}A^{*}
.

(Here
(A_{s}\otimes_{k}A^{*})_{n}:=A_{s}\otimes_{k}(A^{*})_{n-s}
for all
n
.) Moreover,

\mathbf{h}(n)=\mathbf{h}(s-n)
.

Proof.

Plainly, (1) implies (2).

Note (2) and (3) are equivalent owing to (
4.3
). Indeed, in
general, an invertible
k
-module is flat, and its dual is invertible.
Conversely, in general, a locally free
k
-module of finite rank has
rank 1 everywhere if its dual does.

Assume (2). Now,
G_{-s}(A^{*})=G_{-s}(A^{*}\otimes_{A}k)
by (
4.4
)(1)
with
A^{*}
for
C
. However,
G_{-s}(A^{*}\otimes_{A}k)
is a quotient of

A^{*}\otimes_{A}k
. Let
\kappa\colon A^{*}\otimes_{A}k\twoheadrightarrow G_{-s}(A^{*})
be the
quotient map. Now,
G_{-s}(A^{*})
is locally free of rank
\mathbf{h}(s)
,
and
\mathbf{h}(s)\neq 0
. So (2) implies
\kappa
is an isomorphism. Thus
(4) holds.

Assume (4). Then
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(A)=G_{s}A
. Also,
t=1
; that is,

G_{-s}(A^{*})
is invertible. But
G_{-s}(A^{*})=(G_{s}A)^{*}
by
(
2.7
)(3). Thus
(G_{s}A)\otimes_{k}G_{-s}(A^{*})\cong k
.

Choose a
k
-submodule
N\subset A^{*}
that maps isomorphically onto

G_{-s}(A^{*})
. As
A
is level, (
4.4
)(3) implies
A\cdot N=A^{*}
. And, by the above,
(G_{s}A)\otimes_{k}N\cong k
.

So the inclusion
N\hookrightarrow A^{*}
yields a
k
-map
k\to(G_{s}A)\otimes_{k}A^{*}
.
Say 1 maps to
\sum d_{i}\otimes e_{i}
. Define an
A
-map
\varphi\colon A\to(G_{s}A)\otimes_{k}A^{*}
by
\varphi(a):=\sum d_{i}\otimes ae_{i}
. Then
\varphi
is
surjective, as
A\cdot N=A^{*}
. But
A
and
A^{*}
are locally free of
the same rank, and
G_{s}(A)
is invertible. So
\varphi
is an
isomorphism. Thus (5) holds with
L:=G_{s}A
.

Assume (5). Then
k\cong(L\otimes_{k}A^{*})\otimes_{A}k=L\otimes_{k}(A^{*}\otimes_{A}k)
. So

L
is invertible, and
L\cong(A^{*}\otimes_{A}k)^{*}
. But
A\cong L\otimes_{k}A^{*}

in
\mathbb{M}_{A}
by (5), and
L\otimes_{k}A^{*}=(L\otimes_{k}A)\otimes_{A}A^{*}
. Thus (1)
holds. Also,
L\cong\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(A)
by (
4.3
). Thus (A) holds.

In (B), trivially (6)
\Rightarrow
(5). Conversely, take
N:=(A^{*})_{-s}
in
the proof of (4)
\Rightarrow
(5). Then
\varphi\colon A\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}A_{s}\otimes_{k}A^{*}
is
canonical, and
\varphi(A_{n})=A_{s}\otimes_{k}(A^{*})_{n-s}
. Thus (4)
\Rightarrow
(6),
and as
A_{s}
is invertible,
\mathbf{h}(n)=\mathbf{h}^{*}(n-s)=\mathbf{h}(s-n)
.
Thus (B) holds.
∎

(5.4) Example.

Let’s see how a nonstandard weighting can be used to show that a given
graded algebra is not
k
-Gorenstein, thus suggesting a general
question.

Fix variables
X,\,Y
; take
A:=k[X,\,Y]
. Set
I:=\langle X^{2},\,XY,\,Y^{3}\rangle
and
C:=A/I
. Let
x,\,y\in C
denote the residues
of
X,\,Y
. Plainly,
1,\,x,\,y,\,y^{2}
form a free
k
-basis of
C
.
So
C
is
k
-Artinian. Notice that
I
is monomial, so homogeneous
under both the standard and the nonstandard weightings.

Let’s find
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)
. Given
a,\,b,\,c,\,d\in k
, set
\psi:=a+bx+cy+dy^{2}
. Then
x\psi=ax
and
y\psi=ay+cy^{2}
. So
\psi\in\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)
iff
a=0
and
c=0
. Thus
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)=kx+ky^{2}
. Thus

C
is, by (
5.3
)(A)(1)
\Rightarrow
(3), not
k
-Gorenstein.

First, give
X,\,Y
their standard weights of 1. Then the Hilbert
function
\mathbf{h}(n)
has values
1,\,2,\,1
for
n=0,\,1,\,2
; also,

s(\mathbf{h})=2
. Thus
\mathbf{h}(n)=\mathbf{h}(s-n)
although
C
is not

k
-Gorenstein.

Instead, give
X,\,Y
weights of 3, 2. Then the Hilbert function
\mathbf{h}(n)
has values
1,\,0,\,1,\,1,\,1
for
n=0,\,1,\,2,\,3,\,4
; also,

s(\mathbf{h})=4
. Thus
\mathbf{h}(n)\neq\mathbf{h}(s-n)
for
n=1
, and so
C
is,
by (
5.3
)(B), not
k
-Gorenstein.

This example suggests the following question: given a
k
-Artinian
graded
k
-algebra
C
with Hilbert function
\mathbf{h}(n)
where
C
is the
quotient of a polynomial ring by a monomial ideal, but
C
is not

k
-Gorenstein, then is there always a way to weight the variables so
that
\mathbf{h}(n)\neq\mathbf{h}(s-n)
where
s:=s(\mathbf{h})
?

Likely the answer is yes, as Craig Huneke pointed out privately.
Namely,
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)
is generated by residues of monomials
M
, and there
must be at least two
M
by (
5.3
). Likely we can choose weights
so that, with
m:=\max\{\deg(M)\}
, at least two
M
have degree
m
;
then
m=s
by (
4.4
)(2), and so
\mathbf{h}(s)>1=\mathbf{h}(0)
.

(5.5) Example.

Here’s a (fairly standard) example of a
k
-Gorenstein algebra
C
such
that, under the standard weighting,
G_{\bullet}C
is not
k
-Gorenstein.
However, under a nonstandard weighting,
C
is graded, and so
G_{\bullet}C=C
; thus
G_{\bullet}C
is
k
-Gorenstein.

Keep the setup of (
3.7
). Take
r:=2
, so
A:=k[X_{1},\,X_{2}]
. Form the ideal
\mathfrak{M}:=\langle X_{1},\,X_{2}\rangle
, so
A/\mathfrak{M}=k
.
Identify
A^{\dagger}
with
k[X_{1}^{-1},\,X_{2}^{-1}]
.

Set
F:=X_{1}^{-2}+X_{2}^{-3}\in A^{\dagger}
and
D:=AF\subset A^{\dagger}
and

I:=(0:_{A}D)\subset A
. Note

\textstyle\bigl(\sum a_{ij}X_{1}^{i}X_{2}^{j}\bigr)\cdot F=a_{00}F+a_{10}X_{1}^{-1}+a_{01}X_{2}^{-2}+a_{02}X_{2}^{-1}+(a_{20}+a_{03}).

for any
a_{ij}\in k
.
So
\bigl(\sum a_{ij}X_{1}^{i}X_{2}^{j}\bigr)\cdot F=0
iff

a_{00},\,a_{10},\,a_{01},\,a_{02},\,(a_{20}+a_{03})=0
.
Thus
I
is the ideal generated by
X_{1}^{2}-X_{2}^{3},\ X_{1}X_{2}
.

Also
\mathfrak{M}F=kX_{1}^{-1}+kX_{2}^{-2}+kX_{2}^{-1}+k
and
D=kF\oplus\mathfrak{M}F
. Denote the residues in
D/\mathfrak{M}D
of
X_{2}^{-1}
and
F
by

x_{2}^{-1}
and
f
. Thus
D/\mathfrak{M}D=kf=kx_{2}^{-3}
. But
D/\mathfrak{M}D=D\otimes_{A}k
. Thus
D\otimes_{A}k\cong k
. Furthermore,
D\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
.
However,
kF+kX_{2}^{-3}=kX_{1}^{-2}+kX_{2}^{-3}
. Thus
A^{\dagger}/D
is
free over
k
, so projective.

Set
C:=A/I
. Then
C=D^{*}\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
by (
3.5
)(1). So
D=D^{**}=C^{*}
. However,
D:=AF
. So for every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
k
and for every field
K
containing
k/\mathfrak{m}
, the
A_{K}
-module

D_{K}
is cyclic. Thus by (
3.5
)(1), the
k
-algebra
C
is

k
-Gorenstein.

Since
D\otimes_{A}k\cong k
, if
C\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
, then by
(
5.3
)(A)(2)
\Rightarrow
(1), again
C
is
k
-Gorenstein.

To find
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)
, set
E:=k+kX_{1}+kX_{1}^{2}+kX_{2}+kX_{2}^{2}
. Then

E\oplus I=A
. Given
\psi:=a+bX_{1}+cX_{1}^{2}+dX_{2}+eX_{2}^{2}\in E
,
note
X_{1}\psi\in I
iff
a,\,b=0
and
X_{2}\psi\in I
iff
a,\,d,\,e=0
. For
i=1,2
, let
x_{i}\in C
be the residue of
X_{i}
. Thus
whatever the weights
w_{i}
of the
X_{i}
may be,
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)=kx_{1}^{2}=kx_{2}^{3}
. Thus, since
D\otimes_{A}k\cong k
, so is flat, by
(
5.3
)(A)(3)
\Rightarrow
(1), if
C\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
, then, once
again,
C
is
k
-Gorenstein. Alternatively,
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)=kx_{2}^{3}
holds
because of (
4.3
), as
D/\mathfrak{M}D=kx_{2}^{-3}
.

First, weight the
X_{i}
as usual:
w_{1},\,w_{2}:=1
. From (
2.6
),
recall
C\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
iff
G_{\bullet}C\in\mathbb{AG}_{A}
. Note
G_{\bullet}C=G_{\bullet}A\big/G_{\bullet}I
by (
2.5
). But
A
is graded; so
G_{\bullet}A=A
. Correspondingly,
G_{\bullet}I
is generated by the initial forms of
all the elements of
I
. So
X_{1}X_{2},\,X_{1}^{2}\in G_{\bullet}I
. But
X_{2}^{4}\in I
; so
X_{2}^{4}\in G_{\bullet}I
.

It’s not immediately obvious that
X_{1}X_{2},\,X_{1}^{2},\,X_{2}^{4}
generate

G_{\bullet}I
. However, if so, then
G_{\bullet}I
is the
k
-span of the
monomials divisible by at least one of
X_{1}X_{2},\,X_{1}^{2},\,X_{2}^{4}
, and so

G_{\bullet}C\in\mathbb{AG}_{A}
. Moreover, if so, then
x_{1}
and
x_{2}^{3}
lie in

\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(G_{\bullet}C)
, and they are independent over
k
; thus,
G_{\bullet}C

is, by (
5.3
)(A)(1)
\Rightarrow
(3),
not

k
-Gorenstein.

Instead, consider
G_{\bullet}D
. Similarly, it’s the
k
-span of the
initial forms of the elements of
D
. So
G_{\bullet}D=kX_{2}^{-3}+kX_{2}^{-2}+(kX_{1}^{-1}+kX_{2}^{-1})+k
. Hence
D\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
and

G_{\bullet}(A^{\dagger}/D)
is free over
k
. Thus (
3.5
)(2) yields

G_{\bullet}C\in\mathbb{AG}_{A}
. So
C\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
, and thus (
5.3
)(A)
implies, in two different ways, that
C
is
k
-Gorenstein.

The expression for
G_{\bullet}D
above also shows that the residues in

G_{\bullet}D
of
X_{1}^{-1}
and
X_{2}^{-3}
form a free basis of it. Thus
(
4.3
) yields
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(G_{\bullet}C)=kx_{1}\oplus kx_{2}^{3}
.

Also,
G_{\bullet}I:=(0:_{A}G_{\bullet}D)
. But the monomials in
A
and the
inverse (reciprocal) monomials in
A^{\dagger}
form dual bases. So
G_{\bullet}I
is the
k
-span of all the monomials of
A
except

1,\,X_{1},\,X_{2},\,X_{2}^{2},\,X_{2}^{3}
. Thus
X_{1}X_{2},\,X_{1}^{2},\,X_{2}^{4}
do
generate
G_{\bullet}I
, as expected.

In passing, note
\mathfrak{M}\cdot G_{\bullet}D=kX_{2}^{-2}+kX_{2}^{-1}+k
. So

G_{\bullet}D\otimes_{A}k
is free of rank 2 over
k
. Thus, again,
G_{\bullet}C
is not

k
-Gorenstein, this time by (
5.3
)(A)(1)
\Rightarrow
(2), So, by
(
5.2
)(4)
\Rightarrow
(1), although
D
is, by construction,
generated by
F
, nonetheless
G_{\bullet}D
is not generated by the initial
form
X_{2}^{-3}
of
F
, nor by any other single element.

Moreover, no isomorphism
C\cong D
in
\mathbb{M}_{A}
can preserve the
filtrations up to shift; else,
G_{\bullet}C
would be isomorphic in
\mathbb{G}_{A}
to a shift of
G_{\bullet}D
. In particular, the isomorphism
\varphi\colon C\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}D
given by
\varphi(\tau):=\tau f
doesn’t do so. In fact,

(F^{2}C)F=kX_{2}^{-1}+k\subsetneqq F^{-1}D=kX_{1}^{-1}+kX_{2}^{-1}+k.

Lastly, take
w_{1}:=3
and
w_{2}:=2
. Then
F
is weighted homogeneous
of degree
-6
. So the analysis simplifies, as there’s no separate
associated graded case to consider in order to apply (
5.3
).
Indeed, plainly,
(A^{\dagger})_{-6}=D_{-6}\oplus X_{1}^{-2}
and

(A^{\dagger})_{n}=D_{n}
for
-4\leq n\leq 0
, with
D_{-5}=0
and

D_{-1}=0
. So
D
and
A^{\dagger}/D
are free over
k
, and
D\otimes_{A}k=k
. Thus (
5.3
)(A)(2)
\Rightarrow
(1) yields
C
is

k
-Gorenstein.

Alternatively, note that, for every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
k
and for
every field
K
containing
k/\mathfrak{m}
, the generators
X_{1}^{2}-X_{2}^{3},\ X_{1}X_{2}
of
I
map to a regular sequence in
K[X_{1},\,X_{2}]
, because

C_{K}
is Artinian. So
C_{K}
is Gorenstein; see

[
Eis
]
*Cor. 21.19. Furthermore,
C
is
k
-flat by

[
EGAIII0
]
*(10.2.4), p. 19 applied twice. So
C
is
k
-Artinian.
Thus (
5.2
)(2)
\Rightarrow
(1) yields that
C
is
k
-Gorenstein.

(5.6) Proposition
(Linkage)
.

Assume
A
is
k
-Artinian with Hilbert function
\mathbf{h}
, and is

k
-Gorenstein. Let
I\subset A
be an ideal with
C:=A/I\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. Form the ideal
I^{\prime}\subset A

directly linked
to
I
,
namely
I^{\prime}:=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A)
, and form the
A
-submodule of
A^{*}

Macaulay dual
to
C
, namely
C^{*}
. Fix an isomorphism
A\cong\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(A)\otimes_{k}A^{*}
in
\mathbb{M}_{A}
, as provided by (
5.3
)
(A)
.
Set
C^{\prime}:=A/I^{\prime}
.

(1)
Then
I
is directly linked to
I^{\prime}
; that is,
I=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C^{\prime},\,A)
.

(2)
Then
I^{\prime}=\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(A)\otimes_{k}C^{*}
and
C^{\prime}=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(I,\,A)
; moreover,

C^{\prime}\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
.

(3)
Give
C
the induced filtration, and assume
C\in\mathbb{AF}_{A}
. Then

C
is
k
-Gorenstein iff there’s an invertible
k
-module
L
such that

L\otimes_{k}I^{\prime}
is a cyclic
A
-module. If so, then necessarily
L\cong\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)\otimes_{k}\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(A)^{*}
and
L\otimes_{k}I^{\prime}\cong C
.

Proof.

For (1),
given any
k
-module
L
and
A
-modules
M,\,N,\,P
,
form these two canonical maps:

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(M,\,N)\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}\bigl(L\otimes_{k}M,\ L\otimes_{k}N\bigr),

(5.6.1)

\displaystyle L\otimes_{k}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(P,\,N)\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(P,\ L\otimes_{k}N).

(5.6.2)

Assume
L
is invertible. Then, plainly, both maps are isomorphisms.

Take
M:=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(P,\,N)
in (
5.6.1
). Then (
5.6.2
) yields a
canonical isomorphism:

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}\bigl(\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(P,\,N),\ N\bigr)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}\bigl(\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(P,\,L\otimes_{k}N),\ L\otimes_{k}N\bigr).

(5.6.3)

Plainly, (
5.6.3
) respects the two canonical maps from
P
, one to
each side.

Given another
A
-module
Q
and any
A
-isomorphism
\varphi\colon Q\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}L\otimes_{k}N
, note
\varphi
and
\varphi^{-1}
yield an isomorphism:

\psi\colon\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}\bigl(\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(P,\,L\otimes_{k}N),\ L\otimes_{k}N\bigr)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}\bigl(\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(P,\,Q),\ Q)\bigr).

(5.6.4)

Let
\nu_{s}
and
\nu_{t}
denote the canonical maps from
P
to the
source and target of
\psi
. Plainly,
\nu_{t}=\psi\nu_{s}
. Hence, if

\nu_{s}
is an isomorphism, so is
\nu_{t}
. (Also,
\psi=\nu_{t}\nu_{s}^{-1}
; so
\psi
is independent of the choice of
\varphi
.)

Assume
P\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
; take
N:=A^{*}
. Then
P=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}\bigl(\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(P,\,N),\ N)\bigr)
by (
3.3
). Take
L:=\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(A)
; it’s
invertible by (
5.3
)(A)(1)
\Rightarrow
(3). Then
\nu_{s}
is an
isomorphism.

Take
Q:=A
and
\varphi\colon Q\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}L\otimes_{k}N
to be the isomorphism fixed in
the hypotheses. Then
\nu_{t}
is an isomorphism; that is,

P=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}\bigl(\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(P,\,A),\ A)\bigr)
.

Form the exact sequence
0\to I\to A\to C\to 0
. Since
A,\,C\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
, plainly
I\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
. So taking
P:=I
above yields
I=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}\bigl(\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(I,\,A),\ A\bigr)
.

Note
0\to\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A)\to\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(A,\,A)\xrightarrow{\alpha}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(I,\,A)
is
exact. But below
\alpha
is shown to be surjective. Also

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A)=:I^{\prime}
and
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(A,\,A)=A
. Thus
C^{\prime}:=A/I^{\prime}=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(I,\,A)\in\mathbb{AM}_{A}
; hence,
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C^{\prime},\,A)=I
.

The map
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(A,\,A^{*})\to\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(I,\,A^{*})
is surjective, as
A^{*}
is
dualizing for
\mathbb{AM}_{A}
by (
3.3
). And it remains surjective
after applying
L\otimes_{k}\bullet
, as
L
is invertible. But (
5.6.2
) is
functorial in
P
. Hence
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(A,\,L\otimes_{k}A^{*})\to\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(I,\,L\otimes_{k}\,A^{*})
is surjective. Hence the isomorphism

\varphi^{-1}\colon L\otimes_{k}\,A^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}A
induces an isomorphism from that
surjection to
\alpha
. Thus
\alpha
is surjective, as asserted. Thus
(1) holds.

For (2),
note that the quotient map
\kappa\colon A\twoheadrightarrow C
induces
these two embeddings:

\displaystyle L\otimes_{k}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A^{*})\hookrightarrow L\otimes_{k}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(A,\,A^{*})=L\otimes_{k}A^{*}

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,L\otimes_{k}A^{*})\hookrightarrow\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(A,\,L\otimes_{k}A^{*})=L\otimes_{k}A^{*}.

As (
5.6.2
) is an isomorphism that’s compatible with
\kappa
, the
two images are the same submodule
G
of
L\otimes_{k}A^{*}
.

Set
F:=\varphi^{-1}G
. Note
F
is independent of the choice of
\varphi
,
because any two choices differ by an automorphism of
A
, namely, by
multiplication by a unit of
A
as
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(A,A)=A
. Now,
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A^{*})=C^{*}
by (
2.2.2
). And
I^{\prime}:=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A}(C,\,A)
. Thus
(
5.6.2
) induces a canonical isomorphism
I^{\prime}=\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(A)\otimes_{k}C^{*}
,
which is independent of the choice of
\varphi
,. Thus (2) holds.

For (3),
assume
C
is
k
-Gorenstein. Then
(
5.3
)(A)(1)
\Rightarrow
(5) gives an isomorphism
C\cong\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)\otimes_{k}C^{*}
, and
\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)
is invertible by
(
5.3
)(A)(1)
\Rightarrow
(3). So (2) gives
C\cong L\otimes_{k}I^{\prime}
with
L:=\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(C)\otimes_{k}\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(A)^{*}
. Thus
L\otimes_{k}I^{\prime}
is a cyclic
A
-module.

Conversely, assume there’s an invertible
k
-module
L
with
L\otimes_{k}I^{\prime}

cyclic over
A
. Then there’s a surjection
A\twoheadrightarrow L\otimes_{k}I^{\prime}
, and it
factors via a surjection
B\twoheadrightarrow L\otimes_{k}I^{\prime}
, as
L\otimes_{k}I^{\prime}
is a

B
-module. But
L\otimes_{k}I^{\prime}=M\otimes_{k}B^{*}
with
M:=L\otimes_{k}\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(A)

invertible by (2). And
B^{*}
is locally free over
k
of the same rank
as
B
. Hence
B\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}M\otimes_{k}B^{*}
. So
B
is
k
-Gorenstein, and

M\cong\mathop{\rm Soc}\nolimits_{k}(B)
by (
5.3
)(A). Thus (3) holds.
∎

6.
Basic Geometry

(6.1) Setup.

Fix a nonempty Noetherian base scheme
S
, and a nonzero, finitely
generated, graded, quasi-coherent
\mathscr{O}_{S}
-algebra
\mathscr{A}
with
\mathscr{A}_{p}=0
for
p<0
. Given an
S
-scheme
T
, denote by
\mathscr{A}_{T}
the

pullback of
\mathscr{A}
, and by
\mathbb{M}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}
the category of
quasi-coherent
\mathscr{A}_{T}
-modules. For
\mathscr{V}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}
, set

\mathscr{V}^{*}:=\mathop{\mathscr{H}\kern-1.0pt\textit{om\/}}_{T}(\mathscr{V},\,\mathscr{O}_{T})
.

Likewise, generalize all the theory of Sections 2–4 in the standard way
using analogous notation, so that the old theory is recovered over each
open, affine subscheme
U:=\mathop{\rm Spec}(k)
of
S
with
A:=H^{0}(U,\,\mathscr{A})

and over each open, affine subscheme
V:=\mathop{\rm Spec}(K)
of
T
mapping into

U
.

Thus generalizing (
2.2
) and (
2.6
) yields the categories

\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}
, their full subcategories

\mathbb{AF}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}
and
\mathbb{AG}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}
, the (associated-graded)
functor
G_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}\to\mathbb{G}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}
, and its
restriction
G_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{AF}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}\to\mathbb{AG}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}
.

Fix nonzero functions
\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{h}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
with
\mathbf{b}(p)\geq\mathbf{h}(p)\geq 0
for all
p
, and with
\mathbf{h}
finite. Set
s:=s(\mathbf{h}):=\sup\{\,p\mid\mathbf{h}(p)\neq 0\,\}
.

Fix a graded, locally finitely generated
\mathscr{A}
-module
\mathscr{B}
with
graded pieces
\mathscr{B}_{p}
locally free of rank
\mathbf{b}(p)
for all
p
.

Then generalizing (
3.4
) yields the graded dual
\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}
of

\mathscr{B}
and the following sets of subsheaves of
\mathscr{B}_{T}
and
\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}_{T}
:

\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{F}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}\mathbf{H}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}.

(6.1.1)

For convenience, view the elements of
\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}

and
\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
,
not
as certain subsheaves

\mathscr{I}
of
\mathscr{B}_{T}
, but as certain quotients
\mathscr{C}
, via the
canonical bijection
\mathscr{I}\mapsto\mathscr{C}:=\mathscr{B}_{T}/\mathscr{I}
.

As
\mathscr{B}
is locally finitely generated over
\mathscr{A}
and as
S
is
quasi-compact, a finite set of local generators belongs to some

F^{b_{0}}\mathscr{B}
. Then
F^{p}\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{B}
for all
p\leq b_{0}
. So
F^{p}\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}=0
for all
p>-b_{0}
. Fix
b_{0}
as large as possible:

b_{0}:=\sup\{\,p\mid F^{p}\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{B}\,\}=\inf\{\,p\mid b(p)\neq 0\,\}.

(6.1.2)

From (
6.7
) on, assume
\mathscr{A}/F^{1}\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{O}_{S}
, and
generalize the notion of level module in (
4.5
) to sheaves.
Note that
\mathscr{A}_{T}/F^{1}\mathscr{A}_{T}=\mathscr{O}_{T}
for any
T/S
by
(
4.10
)(3).

Finally, given a finite function
\mathbf{t}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
, define

\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}\subset\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}\subset\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}

as the subsets of sheaves of
T
-socle type
\mathbf{t}
.
In particular, define

\mathbf{F}\Lambda_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}\subset\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{H}\Lambda_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}\subset\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}

as the subsets of level sheaves. So
\mathbf{F}\Lambda_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}=\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
and
\mathbf{H}\Lambda_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}=\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h},\mathbf{t}}
where
\mathbf{t}(s):=\mathbf{h}(s)
and
\mathbf{t}(p):=0
for
p\neq s
. By (
4.11
), these
sets behave functorially in
T
.

The statements discussed in the rest of this paper about
\mathscr{B}
and its
quotients and about
\mathbf{h}
and
\mathbf{t}
are plainly equivalent to
similar statements about their shifts by
b_{0}
(to the left).
Therefore, to lighten the notation and to make some statements feel more
familiar to many readers, from now on assume
b_{0}=0
.

(6.2) Theorem.

Set
\ell:=\sum_{p}\mathbf{h}(p)
and
X:=\mathop{\rm Spec}(\mathscr{A})
. Denote the
\mathscr{O}_{X}
-module associated to
\mathscr{B}
by
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}
, and set
Q:=\mathop{\rm{\mathbb{Q}}uot}\nolimits_{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}/X/S}^{\ell}
. Then the sets in (
6.1.1
) form
functors in
T
, which are representable by (possibly empty) subschemes
of
Q
, say
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
,
and
\mathbb{F}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
. Moreover, Macaulay Duality gives canonical
isomorphisms
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}=\mathbb{F}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}=\mathbb{H}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
.

Proof.

To begin, fix an
S
-map
T\to R
. Let
\mathscr{C}
be an
\mathscr{A}_{R}
-quotient of
\mathscr{B}_{R}
with the induced filtration. By right
exactness of pullback, for all
q
, these two sequences

(F^{q}(\mathscr{B}_{R}))_{T}\to(F^{q}\mathscr{C})_{T}\to 0(F^{q}\mathscr{C})_{T}\to\mathscr{C}_{T}\to(\mathscr{C}/F^{q}\mathscr{C})_{T}\to 0

are exact. But
(F^{q}(\mathscr{B}_{R}))_{T}=\bigl(\bigoplus_{p\geq q}(\mathscr{B}_{p})_{R}\bigr)_{T}=\bigoplus_{p\geq q}(\mathscr{B}_{p})_{T}=F^{q}(\mathscr{B}_{T})
. Therefore,

\mathscr{C}_{T}/F^{q}(\mathscr{C}_{T})=(\mathscr{C}/F^{q}\mathscr{C})_{T}.

(6.2.1)

But
0\to G_{q}(\mathscr{C}_{T})\to\mathscr{C}_{T}/F^{q+1}(\mathscr{C}_{T})\to\mathscr{C}_{T}/F^{q}(\mathscr{C}_{T})\to 0
is exact. Thus, so is

0\to G_{q}(\mathscr{C}_{T})\to(\mathscr{C}/F^{q+1}\mathscr{C})_{T}\to(\mathscr{C}/F^{q}\mathscr{C})_{T}\to 0.

(6.2.2)

If
\mathscr{C}/F^{q}\mathscr{C}
is flat over
R
, then
0\to G_{q}\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}/F^{q+1}\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}/F^{q}\mathscr{C}\to 0
remains exact on pullback to

T
, and so (
6.2.2
) yields

G_{q}(\mathscr{C}_{T})=(G_{q}\mathscr{C})_{T}.

(6.2.3)

Suppose now
\mathscr{C}\in\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{R}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Then
\mathscr{C}\in\mathbb{AF}_{\mathscr{A}_{R}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. So (
2.7
)(1) implies
\mathscr{C}
and

\mathscr{C}/F^{p}\mathscr{C}
are flat over
R
. Moreover,
\mathscr{C}_{T}\in\mathbb{AF}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
as
G_{q}(\mathscr{C}_{T})
is locally free of rank
\mathbf{h}(q)
for
all
q
by (
6.2.2
). Thus
\mathscr{C}_{T}\in\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Plainly, if
\mathscr{C}\in\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{R}}^{\mathbf{h}}
,
then
\mathscr{C}_{T}\in\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Thus as
T
varies,

\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
and
\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}

form functors.

Next, form
X\times_{S}Q=\mathop{\rm Spec}(\mathscr{A}_{Q})
. Let
\mathscr{Q}
be the
\mathscr{A}_{Q}
-quotient module of
\mathscr{B}_{Q}
whose associated
\mathscr{O}_{X\times_{S}Q}
-module is the universal quotient of
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{Q}
. Give

\mathscr{Q}
the induced filtration. For each
q
, set
\ell_{q}:=\sum_{p<q}\mathbf{h}(p)
. By the theory of flattening stratification
(
[
SB221
]
*Lem. 3.6, p. 15), there’s a subscheme
Q_{q}
of
Q

such that an
S
-map
T\to Q
factors through
Q_{q}
iff
(\mathscr{Q}/F^{q}\mathscr{Q})_{T}
is locally free of rank
\ell_{q}
. Set

\textstyle\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}:=\bigcap_{q}Q_{q}\subset Q.

We have to prove that its set of
T
-points is (canonically) equal to

\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
.

Given
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
, note
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{AF}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. So (
2.7
)(1) implies
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
and
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}/F^{q}\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
are locally free of ranks
r
and
\ell_{q}

for all
q
. So there’s a unique map
T\to Q
such that
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}=\mathscr{Q}_{T}
. Hence (
6.2.1
), with
Q
and
\mathscr{Q}
for
R
and
\mathscr{C}
,
implies
(\mathscr{Q}/F^{q}\mathscr{Q})_{T}
is locally free of rank
\ell_{q}
for all

q
. Thus
T\to Q
factors through
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
.

Conversely, suppose
T\to Q
factors through
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Then
\mathscr{Q}_{T}\in\mathbb{AF}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
owing to
(
6.2.1
) with
Q
and
\mathscr{Q}
for
R
and
\mathscr{C}
. Thus
\mathscr{C}\in\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Thus
\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
is equal to the set of
T
-points of
\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
, as
desired.

Let’s now construct
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. For convenience, set

Z:=\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Define
\mathscr{S}\subset\mathscr{B}
by

B_{Q}/\mathscr{S}:=\mathscr{Q}
; give
\mathscr{S}
the induced filtration. Consider the
pullback
\mathscr{Q}_{Z}
of
\mathscr{Q}
and its associated graded quotient
G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Q}_{Z})
. Note
\mathscr{S}_{Z}\subset\mathscr{B}_{Z}
and
\mathscr{B}_{Z}/\mathscr{S}_{Z}=\mathscr{Q}_{Z}
as
\mathscr{Q}
is flat. So
G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{Z})\subset\mathscr{B}_{Z}
and
\mathscr{B}_{Z}/G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{Z})=G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Q}_{Z})
by (
2.5
). Set
\mathscr{R}:={\mathscr{B}}_{Z}/(\mathscr{S}_{Z}+G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{Z}))
.

The theory of flattening stratification yields a subscheme
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
of
Z
such that, given a map
T\to Z
, it factors through

\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
iff
\mathscr{R}_{T}
is locally free of rank

\ell
. We have to prove that the set of
T
-points of
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
is (canonically) equal to
\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
.

Recall
\mathscr{S}\subset\mathscr{B}
and
B_{Q}/\mathscr{S}:=\mathscr{Q}
. But
\mathscr{Q}
is
flat; so
\mathscr{S}_{T}\subset\mathscr{B}_{T}
and
\mathscr{B}_{T}/\mathscr{S}_{T}=\mathscr{Q}_{T}
.
So (
2.5
) yields
G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{T})\subset\mathscr{B}_{T}
and
\mathscr{B}_{T}/G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{T})=G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Q}_{T})
. In particular,
G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{Z})\subset\mathscr{B}_{Z}
and
\mathscr{B}_{Z}/G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{Z})=G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Q}_{Z})
. Now,

\mathscr{Q}_{Z}/F^{q}\mathscr{Q}_{Z}
is flat for all
q
by the definition of
Z
; so

(G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Q}_{Z}))_{T}=G_{\bullet}((\mathscr{Q}_{Z})_{T})
by (
6.2.3
), and

G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Q}_{Z})
is flat by (
6.2.2
) with
Z
for
T
.
Hence
(G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{Z}))_{T}\subset\mathscr{B}_{T}
and
\mathscr{S}_{T}\subset\mathscr{B}_{T}
. Furthermore,

\mathscr{B}_{T}\big/(G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{Z}))_{T}=(G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Q}_{Z}))_{T}=G_{\bullet}((\mathscr{Q}_{Z})_{T})=G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Q}_{T})=\mathscr{B}_{T}/G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{T}).

Thus
(G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{Z}))_{T}=G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{T})\subset\mathscr{B}_{T}
.

Note the following right exact sequence:

\bigl(\mathscr{S}_{Z}+G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{Z})\bigr)_{T}\to\mathscr{B}_{T}\to\mathscr{R}_{T}\to 0
.
But
(\mathscr{S}_{Z})_{T}=\mathscr{S}_{T}\subset\mathscr{B}_{T}
and
(G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{Z}))_{T}=G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{T})\subset\mathscr{B}_{T}
. Thus
\mathscr{R}_{T}=\mathscr{B}_{T}/(\mathscr{S}_{T}+G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{T}))
.

Given
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}\subset\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
, there’s a unique map
T\to Z
with
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}=(\mathscr{Q}_{Z})_{T}
. But
(\mathscr{Q}_{Z})_{T}=\mathscr{Q}_{T}=\mathscr{B}_{T}/\mathscr{S}_{T}
and

G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{Q}_{T})=\mathscr{B}_{T}/G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{T})
. Further,
G_{\bullet}\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}=\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
as
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Hence
\mathscr{S}_{T}=G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{T})\subset\mathscr{B}_{T}
. So
\mathscr{R}_{T}=\mathscr{B}_{T}/\mathscr{S}_{T}
. So

\mathscr{R}_{T}
is locally free of rank
\ell
. Thus
T\to Z
factors through

\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
, as desired.

Conversely, given a map
T\to\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
, note
\mathscr{R}_{T}

is locally free of rank
\ell
by definition of
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{B}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Form the natural surjections
\mathscr{B}_{T}/\mathscr{S}_{T}\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{R}_{T}
and

\mathscr{B}_{T}/G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{T})\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{R}_{T}
. Recall
\mathscr{B}_{T}/\mathscr{S}_{T}=Q_{T}
,
and as
T\to\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}\subset\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
, also
\mathscr{B}_{T}/G_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}_{T})=G_{\bullet}((\mathscr{Q}_{Z})_{T})
.
Moreover, both
Q_{T}
and
G_{\bullet}((\mathscr{Q}_{Z})_{T})
are locally free of rank

\ell
. So both surjections are isomorphisms. So
Q_{T}=G_{\bullet}(Q_{T})
.
Thus
\mathscr{Q}_{T}\in\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
, as desired.

Alternatively,
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
may be constructed as follows.
Set
\mathscr{C}_{p}:=\mathop{\rm Im}\bigl((\mathscr{B}_{p})_{Q}\to\mathscr{Q}\bigr)
for all
p
. By
the theory of flattening stratification, there’s a subscheme
Y_{p}
of

Q
such that a map
T\to Q
factors through
Y_{p}
iff
(\mathscr{Q}/\mathscr{C}_{p})_{T}
is locally free of rank
\ell-\mathbf{h}(p)
. Set
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}:=\bigcap_{p}Y_{p}\subset Q
. We have to prove its set of

T
-points is equal to
\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
.

Suppose
T\to Q
factors through
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Then

(\mathscr{Q}/\mathscr{C}_{p})_{T}
is locally free of rank
\ell-\mathbf{h}(p)
. Set
\mathscr{C}_{p}^{\prime}:=\mathop{\rm Im}\bigl((\mathscr{B}_{p})_{T}\to\mathscr{Q}_{T}\bigr)
. By right exactness of
pullback, the sequences

(\mathscr{B}_{p})_{T}\to(\mathscr{C}_{p})_{T}\to 0(\mathscr{C}_{p})_{T}\to\mathscr{Q}_{T}\to(\mathscr{Q}/\mathscr{C}_{p})_{T}\to 0

are exact. So
\mathscr{Q}_{T}/\mathscr{C}_{p}^{\prime}=(\mathscr{Q}/\mathscr{C}_{p})_{T}
. So
\mathscr{Q}_{T}/\mathscr{C}_{p}^{\prime}
is locally free of rank
\ell-\mathbf{h}(p)
. Thus
\mathscr{C}_{p}^{\prime}
is locally
free of rank
\mathbf{h}(p)
.

So
\bigoplus_{p}\mathscr{C}_{p}^{\prime}
is locally free of rank
\ell
. However, the
canonical map
\bigoplus_{p}\mathscr{C}_{p}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{Q}_{T}
is surjective; so it’s
an isomorphism. Thus
\mathscr{Q}_{T}
is a homogeneous quotient of
\mathscr{B}_{T}
,
and
\mathscr{Q}_{T}\in\mathbb{AG}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}\subset\mathbb{AF}_{\mathscr{A}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Thus
\mathscr{C}\in\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
.

Conversely, given
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
, note
\mathscr{Q}_{p}^{\prime}:=\mathop{\rm Im}\bigl((\mathscr{B}_{p})_{T}\to\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\bigr)
. As
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
, there’s a unique map
T\to Q
such that

\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}=\mathscr{Q}_{T}
. As above,
\mathscr{Q}_{T}/\mathscr{Q}_{p}^{\prime}=(\mathscr{Q}/\mathscr{C}_{p})_{T}
.
But
\mathscr{Q}_{p}^{\prime}
is locally free of rank
\mathbf{h}(p)
as
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Hence
(\mathscr{Q}/\mathscr{C})_{T}
is locally free of
rank
\ell-\mathbf{h}(p)
. Thus
T\to Q
factors through
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Thus
\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
is equal to the set
of
T
-points of
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
, as desired.

Finally, owing to (
3.5
),
\mathscr{C}\mapsto\mathscr{C}^{*}
defines a
bijection
\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\mathbf{F}\Delta_{\mathscr{(}B_{T})^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
, which restricts to a bijection
\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\mathbf{H}\Delta_{\mathscr{(}B_{T})^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
. These
bijections are functorial in
T
owing to (
4.10.1
). Take
\mathbb{F}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}:=\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
and
\mathbb{H}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}:=\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Thus
\mathbf{F}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
and
\mathbf{H}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}^{\dagger}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}^{*}}
form functors in
T
, which are representable by
subschemes of
Q
.
∎

(6.3) Corollary.

There’s a canonical pair of maps consisting of a closed embedding
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
and a retraction

\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Moreover,
there’s an analogous pair of maps
\mathbb{H}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
and
\mathbb{F}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{H}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Both pairs respect Macaulay duality.

Proof.

For each
S
-scheme
T
, by definition,
\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
is a subset of
\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
; plainly, the
inclusion is functorial in
T
. Furthermore, taking associated graded
modules gives a retraction
\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}\to\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
; owing to (
6.2.3
), it’s functorial in

T
.

As the functors are representable by quasi-projective
S
-schemes by
(
6.2
), the maps between the functors are representable by
separated maps of schemes, say
\iota\colon\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}\to\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
and
\gamma\colon\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}\to\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
.

Moreover,
\gamma\iota=1
as
\gamma\iota
represents the identity map
of the functor
T\mapsto\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Thus
\iota

is a closed embedding by
[
EGAI
]
*(I, 5.2.9) as
\gamma\iota
is
one and
\gamma
is separated.

Similarly, there’s an analogous pair
\mathbb{H}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
and
\mathbb{F}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{H}\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}
.

Finally, owing to (
3.5
), the two pairs respect Macaulay
duality.
∎

(6.4) Proposition.

Assume
\mathscr{B}
is an
S
-Artinian and
S
-Gorenstin quotient of
\mathscr{A}

of socle degree
b
. For all
p
, set
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}(p):=\mathbf{b}(p)-\mathbf{h}(b-p)
. Define a (Gorenstein direct)
linkage
map
\lambda^{\mathbf{h}}\colon\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}}\to\mathbb{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}}
on

T
-points
\mathscr{C}\in\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}}
by
\lambda^{\mathbf{h}}(\mathscr{C}):=\mathscr{B}/\mathscr{I}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}}
where

\mathscr{I}^{\prime}:=\mathop{\mathscr{H}\kern-1.0pt\textit{om\/}}_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}(\mathscr{C},\,\mathscr{B}_{T})
.

(1)
Then
\lambda^{\mathbf{h}}
is an isomorphism,
(\lambda^{\mathbf{h}})^{-1}=\lambda^{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}}
, and
\lambda^{\mathbf{h}}(\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}})=\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}}
.

(2)
Fix
R:=\mathop{\rm Spec}(K)/S
where
K
is a field, and fix
\mathscr{C}\in\mathbf{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{R}}^{\mathbf{h}}
. Assume the ideal in
\mathscr{A}_{R}
of
\mathscr{B}_{R}
is generated by a homogeneous regular sequence. Then
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathbf{h}}
is
S
-smooth at
\mathscr{C}
iff
\mathbb{H}\Psi_{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}}
is
S
-smooth at
\lambda^{\mathbf{h}}(\mathscr{C})
.

Proof.

For (1),
note that
\lambda^{\mathbf{h}}
is a well-defined map of
functors, as follows. First,
\lambda^{\mathbf{h}}(\mathscr{C})\in\mathbb{AM}_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}
owing to (
5.6
)(2). But plainly,
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}
is the Hilbert
function of
\lambda^{\mathbf{h}}(\mathscr{C})
. Thus
\lambda^{\mathbf{h}}(\mathscr{C})\in\mathbf{F}\Psi_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}^{\mathbf{h}^{\prime}}
. Now, both
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{B}_{T}
are
locally free of finite rank; so, given
T^{\prime}/T
, the base-change map

\mathop{\mathscr{H}\kern-1.0pt\textit{om\/}}_{\mathscr{B}_{T}}(\mathscr{C},\,\mathscr{B}_{T})_{T^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathscr{H}\kern-1.0pt\textit{om\/}}_{\mathscr{B}_{T^{\prime}}}(\mathscr{C}_{T^{\prime}},\,\mathscr{B}_{T^{\prime}})
is an isomorphism. Thus
